Several terms redirect here. For other uses, see [Catholic Church (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_\(disambiguation\)), [Catholic (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_\(disambiguation\)), [Catholicism (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholicism_\(disambiguation\)), and [Roman Catholic Church (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church_\(disambiguation\)).

The **Catholic Church** ([Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *Ecclesia Catholica*), also called the **Roman Catholic Church** (Latin: *Ecclesia Catholica Romana*),[^cite_note-8] is the [largest Christian church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations_by_number_of_members), with an estimated 1.28 to 1.41 billion [baptized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism#Catholicism) members [worldwide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_by_country) as of 2026. It consists of 24 autonomous (*[sui iuris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sui_iuris)*) churches. The largest one of these churches is the [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church). Besides it, there are 23 smaller [Eastern Catholic Churches](214903.md). Together, the Latin Church and Eastern Catholic churches are organized into nearly 3,500 [dioceses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese) and [eparchies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eparchy) governed by [bishops](8799424.md). Throughout history, the church has had a large role in the development of [Western civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_civilization). Catholic communities are present worldwide through [missions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_missions), [immigration](4599312.md), and [conversions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conversion_to_Christianity). The majority of Catholics live in the [Global South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_North_and_Global_South), reflecting rapid demographic growth in [Africa](5334607.md), [Asia](689.md), and [Latin America](18524.md), as well as [secularization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularization) in parts of [Europe](9239.md) and [North America](21139.md).

[Catholic doctrine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_theology) is rooted in the [Nicene Creed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Creed) and holds that the church is the "[one, holy, catholic and apostolic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One,_holy,_catholic_and_apostolic_Church)" founded by [Jesus Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_in_Christianity).[^cite_note-Catholic_News_Service-9][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter20047-10][^cite_note-13] It teaches that [bishops](4092.md) are the [successors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_succession) of the [apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament) and that the [pope](23056.md)—the bishop of Rome—is the successor of [Saint Peter the Apostle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter), entrusted with a unique and [primary pastoral role](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primacy_of_Peter). [Leo XIV](54520372.md) is the current pope, serving as [the head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_supremacy) of the church; the [Diocese of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_Rome) forms his local jurisdiction, while the [Holy See](13393.md) serves as the church's central governing authority through the [Roman Curia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Curia). Apostolic teaching is understood to be transmitted through [Scripture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biblical_canon) and [sacred tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_tradition), interpreted by the [magisterium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magisterium), the church's teaching authority. Catholic [liturgical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical) life includes the [Roman Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Rite), [other rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_liturgical_rites) of the Latin Church, and the liturgical traditions of the Eastern Catholic Churches. Religious orders, [monastic communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enclosed_religious_orders), and lay [movements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Association_of_the_Christian_faithful) contribute to a wide [range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_particular_churches_and_liturgical_rites) of [theological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theological) and [devotional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_devotions) expressions within Catholicism globally.[^cite_note-14][^cite_note-Gunton2-15]

Among the church's [seven sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacraments_of_the_Catholic_Church), the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist_in_the_Catholic_Church) is seen as the source and summit of the Christian life and is celebrated in the [Mass](5684685.md). Catholics believe that through [consecration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecration#Eucharist) by a [priest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priesthood_\(Catholic_Church\)), the [bread](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramental_bread) and [wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramental_wine) become the [body and blood of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transubstantiation). The [Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virgin_Mary) is [venerated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church) as the [Mother of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_God) and honored through [dogmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogma_in_the_Catholic_Church) such as the [Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immaculate_Conception), [perpetual virginity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perpetual_virginity), and [Assumption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assumption_of_Mary), including [devotional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_devotions) practices.[^cite_note-marian_dogmas-16] [Catholic social teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_social_teaching) emphasizes care for the poor, the sick, and the [marginalized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marginalized). The church operates tens of thousands of [educational](1452366.md), [medical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_health_care), and [charitable](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_charities) institutions worldwide, making it the largest non-governmental provider of education and health care.

Its relations with other Christian traditions have been shaped by divisions. The separation between the church and [Eastern Orthodox churches](10186.md) within the [state Roman church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_as_the_Roman_state_religion) developed [gradually](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) and was solidified by the [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade), amidst theological and political disputes, especially over papal authority.[^cite_note-17] Earlier schisms occurred with the [Church of the East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_the_East) after the [Council of Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ephesus) (431) and with the [Oriental Orthodox Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches) following the [Council of Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon) (451). The 16th century [Protestant Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation) led to [new Christian traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) and prompted the Catholic [Counter‑Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Reformation). Since the late 20th century, the church has faced criticism on its teachings on [sexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_theology_of_sexuality), [clerical celibacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clerical_celibacy_in_the_Catholic_Church), the [ordination of women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordination_of_women_and_the_Catholic_Church), and its handling of [clerical sexual abuse](158934.md).[^cite_note-18]

## Name

Further information: [Catholic (term)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_\(term\)) and [Roman Catholic (term)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_\(term\))



The first use of the term "Catholic Church", meaning "universal church", was by the [Church Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Father) Saint [Ignatius of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignatius_of_Antioch) in his *[Letter to the Smyrnaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Letter_to_the_Smyrnaeans)* in c. 110 AD.

[^cite_note-19]

&#x20;Ignatius of Antioch also is credited with the first recorded use of the term *[Christianity](5211.md)* ten years earlier, in c. 100 AD.

[^cite_note-20]

&#x20;He died in Rome, with his [relics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Relic) located in [San Clemente al Laterano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Clemente_al_Laterano).

Catholic (from [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): καθολικός, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek): *katholikos*, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'universal') is first attested as an adjective used to describe the church in the early second century.[^cite_note-21] The first known use of the phrase "the catholic church" ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): καθολικὴ ἐκκλησία, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek): *katholikḕ ekklēsía*) appears in a letter written around AD 100 by [Ignatius of Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Ignatius_of_Antioch) to the [Smyrnaeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smyrna),[^cite_note-22] which reads: "Wheresoever the bishop shall appear, there let the people be, even as where Jesus may be, there is the universal \[katholike] Church."[^cite_note-CathEnc1910_Catholic-23] In the *Catechetical Lectures* (c. 350) of [Cyril of Jerusalem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Cyril_of_Jerusalem), the name "Catholic Church" was used to distinguish it from other groups that also called themselves "the church".[^cite_note-CathEnc1910_Catholic-23][^cite_note-24] The "Catholic" notion was further emphasized in the edict *[De fide catolica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Thessalonica)*, issued in 380 by [Theodosius I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theodosius_I), the last emperor to rule over both the [eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Roman_Empire) and [western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Roman_Empire) halves of the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), when he established the [state church of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_church_of_the_Roman_Empire).[^cite_note-25]

Since the [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) of 1054, the [Eastern Orthodox Church](10186.md) has taken the adjective "Orthodox" as its distinctive epithet; its official name continues to be the "Orthodox Catholic Church."[^cite_note-26] The [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church) was described as "Catholic", with that description also denoting those in communion with the [Holy See](13393.md) after the [Protestant Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformation) of the 16th century, when those who ceased to be in communion became known as Protestants.[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-McBrien-28]

While the "Roman Church" has been used to describe the pope's [Diocese of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese_of_Rome) since the [Fall of the Western Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_the_Western_Roman_Empire) and into the [Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages) (6th–10th century), "Roman Catholic Church" has been applied to the whole church in the English language since the Protestant Reformation in the late 16th century.[^cite_note-29] Further, some refer to the Latin Church as "Roman Catholic" in distinction from the Eastern Catholic churches.[^cite_note-30] "Roman Catholic" has occasionally appeared in documents produced by the Holy See,[^cite_note-RCHolySeeR-31] and has been used by certain national [episcopal conferences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_conference) and local dioceses.[^cite_note-RCbishop-32]

The name "Catholic Church" for the whole church is used in the *[Catechism of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church)* (1990) and the [*Code of Canon Law*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Code_of_Canon_Law) (1983). "Catholic Church" is also used in the documents of the [Second Vatican Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council) (1962–1965),[^cite_note-33] the [First Vatican Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Vatican_Council) (1869–1870),[^cite_note-34] the [Council of Trent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Trent) (1545–1563)[^cite_note-35] and numerous other official documents.[^cite_note-36][^cite_note-37] The *[Free Dictionary by Farlex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Free_Dictionary)* suggests that the abbreviation "RCC" has some informal use to indicate the Roman Catholic Church.[^cite_note-38]

## History

Main article: [History of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Catholic_Church)

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_Catholic_Church).

Further information: [Historiography of early Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historiography_of_early_Christianity)

See also: [History of Catholic theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Catholic_theology)

### Apostolic era and papacy

Main article: [Apostolic Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Age)

Painting a haloed Jesus Christ passing keys to a kneeling man.

A c. 1481–1482 [fresco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresco) by [Pietro Perugino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pietro_Perugino) in the [Sistine Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sistine_Chapel) showing [Jesus](1095706.md) giving the [keys of heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keys_of_heaven) to [Saint Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter)



[*The Last Supper*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Last_Supper_\(Leonardo\)) in the [Santa Maria delle Grazie Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Maria_delle_Grazie,_Milan) in [Milan](36511.md), Italy, a late 1490s mural painting by [Leonardo da Vinci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci), depicting the [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper) of Jesus and his [twelve apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twelve_apostles), depicts the [final meal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper) of [Jesus](1095706.md) before [his crucifixion and death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus).

The [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament), particularly the [Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospels), records Jesus' activities and teaching, his appointment of the [Twelve Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament), and his [Great Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Commission) to them, instructing them to continue his work.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter200430-39][^cite_note-Kreeft98O-40] The *[Acts of Apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Apostles)* recounts the founding of the [Christian Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Church) and the spread of its message throughout the Roman Empire.[^cite_note-41] The Catholic Church teaches that its public ministry began on [Pentecost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecost), which occurred fifty days after the date on which Christ is believed to have [risen from the dead](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Christ).[^cite_note-Barry48-42] At Pentecost, the apostles are held to have received the [Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Spirit_in_Christianity), preparing them for their mission of leading the church.[^cite_note-43][^cite_note-44] The Catholic Church further teaches that the [college of bishops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_bishops), led by the [bishop of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishop_of_Rome), is the [successor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_succession) to the apostles.[^cite_note-45]

In the account of the [Confession of Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confession_of_Peter) found in the [Gospel of Matthew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew), Christ designates Peter as the "rock" upon which his Church will be built.[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] The Catholic Church considers the bishop of Rome, the pope, to be the successor of [Saint Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter).[^cite_note-48] Some scholars hold that Peter was the first bishop of Rome,[^cite_note-JoyceCE1913-49] while other scholars argue that the institution of the papacy does not depend on the view that Peter was bishop of Rome, or even on the claim that he ever resided in Rome.[^cite_note-50]

Many scholars maintain that a church structure consisting of multiple presbyters/bishops persisted in Rome until the mid-2nd century, when a structure with a single bishop and multiple presbyters was adopted, and that later writers retrospectively applied the title "bishop of Rome" to the most prominent members of the clergy in the earlier period, as well as to Peter himself.[^cite_note-REB-51] On this basis, [Bart D. Ehrman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bart_D._Ehrman) argues Peter "could not have been the first bishop of Rome", while also noting that the church "did not have anyone as its bishop until about a hundred years after Peter's death."[^cite_note-ehrman-52] [Raymond E. Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raymond_E._Brown) likewise states that it is anachronistic to speak of Peter in terms of a local bishop of Rome, but that Christians of that period would have regarded Peter as exercising roles that contributed "to the development of the role of the papacy in the subsequent church." These roles, Brown argues, "contributed enormously to seeing the bishop of Rome ... as the successor of Peter ... for the church universal."[^cite_note-REB-51]

### Antiquity and Roman Empire

Main articles: [Early Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Christianity), [Pentarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentarchy), and [List of heresies in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_heresies_in_the_Catholic_Church)



A 19th-century illustration by [Henry William Brewer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_William_Brewer) of [Old St. Peter's Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_St._Peter's_Basilica), built in 318 by [Constantine the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great)

The [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire) facilitated the spread of new ideas through its extensive network of roads and waterways, the relative security of the *[Pax Romana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pax_Romana)*, and the promotion of a common culture with strong Greek influences, all of which allowed ideas to be more easily expressed and understood.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter200424-53] However, unlike most religions in the Roman Empire, Christianity required its followers to renounce [all other gods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Idolatry), a practice inherited from [Judaism](15624.md). Because Christians refused to participate in [pagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paganism) festivals and civic rituals, they were excluded from many aspects of public life, leading some non-Christians, including government authorities, to fear that they were angering the gods and thereby threatening the peace and prosperity of the empire. The [resulting prosecutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_Roman_Empire) became a defining element of early Christian self-understanding until Christianity was legalized in the 4th century.[^cite_note-macculloch155and164-54]

In 313, [Constantine the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantine_the_Great)—the first Roman emperor to convert to Christianity—issued the [Edict of Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Milan), which legalized the Christian faith, and he moved the imperial capital to [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constantinople) (modern [Istanbul, Turkey](3391396.md)) in 330. In 380, the [Edict of Thessalonica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Thessalonica) made [Nicene Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicene_Christianity) the [state church of the Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_church_of_the_Roman_Empire), a status that continued within the shrinking territory of the [Byzantine Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Empire) until its [fall in 1453](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_Constantinople). Elsewhere, the church functioned independently of imperial authority, becoming especially evident after the [East-West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism). During the period of the [Seven Ecumenical Councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_seven_ecumenical_councils), five principal sees emerged—Rome, Constantinople, [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria), [Antioch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch), and [Jerusalem](16043.md)—formalized in the mid-6th century, arranged by [Justinian I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I), the [Byzantine emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_emperor) from 527 to 565, as the pentarchy.[^cite_note-Valliere2012-55][^cite_note-Bartholomew2008-56]

In 451, the [Council of Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Chalcedon), in a canon of disputed validity,[^cite_note-Michalopulos-57] elevated the [see of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/See_of_Constantinople) to a role "second in eminence and power to the bishop of Rome."[^cite_note-Noble214-58] From c. 350 – c. 500, the bishops, or popes, of Rome steadily increased their authority through consistently intervening in helping [orthodox leaders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_see) during theological disputes, which encouraged appeals to them.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-59] Emperor [Justinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justinian_I), under his controlled territories, established a form of [caesaropapism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caesaropapism) where he could regulate "the minutest details of worship and discipline" and "theologian opinions" in the church—establishing imperial influence over Rome and other Western territories again.[^cite_note-60] This act created the [Byzantine Papacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Papacy) period (537–752) in which popes required approval from the emperor or his representative for consecration, leading to most being selected by the emperor from his Greek-speaking subjects, which created a "melting pot" of Western and Eastern Christian traditions in art and liturgy.[^cite_note-61][^cite_note-62]

In the following centuries, Germanic tribes who invaded the Roman Empire adopted Christianity in its [Arian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arianism) form, which the [Council of Nicaea](11118.md) declared [heretical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heresy), causing discord between Germanic rulers and Catholic subjects.[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64] In 497, [Clovis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clovis_I), the [Frankish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankish_language) ruler, converted to orthodox Catholicism and he aligned himself with the papacy and the [monastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monasticism) communities—an act that unified Germanic rulers and Catholic subjects for the most part.[^cite_note-65] Following his lead, the [Visigoths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visigoths) converted to Catholicism in 589, and the [Lombards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lombards) in Italy gradually adopted it during the 7th century.[^cite_note-66][^cite_note-67]

[Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity)—particularly through its monastic institutions—played a massive role in preserving [classical civilization](252905.md), including its [artistic traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illuminated_manuscript) and literacy.[^cite_note-Cahill_Thomas-68] [Benedict of Nursia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedict_of_Nursia) (c. 480–543), one of the founders of [Western monasticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_monasticism), with his *[Rule](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rule_of_Saint_Benedict)*, exerted a crucial influence on European culture with his appropriation of the church's monastic spiritual heritage and his preservation and transmission of ancient culture with the spread of the [Benedictine](4240.md) tradition. During this time, monastic [Ireland](147575.md) became a center of scholarship; early Irish missionaries such as [Columbanus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbanus) and [Columba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columba) spread Christianity and established monasteries across [continental Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_Europe).[^cite_note-Cahill_Thomas-68]

### Middle Ages and Renaissance

Further information: [History of Christianity during the Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Christianity_during_the_Middle_Ages) and [Christianity in the 16th century § Renaissance Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_16th_century#Renaissance_Church)



An interior view of the [Sainte-Chapelle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sainte-Chapelle) on the [Île de la Cité](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%8Ele_de_la_Cit%C3%A9) in [Paris](22989.md), France, completed in 1248. During the Middle Ages, many buildings in the [Gothic architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture)-style were erected as places of worship for the Catholic Church.

From [Late Antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_antiquity) to the dawn of the [modern age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_era), the Catholic Church held a dominant influence on Western civilization, being the primary sponsor of [Romanesque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Romanesque_art_and_architecture), [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_art), [Renaissance](311401.md), [Mannerist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mannerism), and [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque) styles in art, architecture, and music—including visual artists like [Raphael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael), [Michelangelo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelangelo), [Leonardo da Vinci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonardo_da_Vinci), [Sandro Botticelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandro_Botticelli), [Fra Angelico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fra_Angelico), [Tintoretto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tintoretto), [Titian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titian), [Gian Lorenzo Bernini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gian_Lorenzo_Bernini), and [Caravaggio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caravaggio).[^cite_note-O'CollinsPref-69][^cite_note-70][^cite_note-71] A [Stanford University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford_University) historian Paul Legutko said the church is "at the center of the development of the values, ideas, science, laws, and institutions" of [Western civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture).[^cite_note-72]

Monks established the [first universities in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_university) in the time of Western [Christendom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christendom).[^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74][^cite_note-75] In [higher education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_education), several older cathedral schools became universities beginning in the 11th century, including the [University of Oxford](31797.md), [University of Paris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Paris), and [University of Bologna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Bologna). Dating back to 6th-century AD, [monks](419369.md) and [nuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nun) spearheaded higher education with Christian cathedral schools or monastic schools.[^cite_note-auto-76] These new universities expanded their curriculums to include academic programs for clerics, lawyers, civil servants, and physicians.[^cite_note-77] Thus, due to its initial origins, the university is generally regarded as starting in a [Medieval Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_Christian) setting.[^cite_note-verger1999-78][^cite_note-79][^cite_note-Rüegg,_Walter_1992,_pp._XIX-80]

Massive [mid-7th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_7th_century) Islamic invasions elongated the struggles of power between Christianity and Islam throughout the [Mediterranean Basis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_basin); the Byzantine Empire lost its lands of the eastern patriarchates of Jerusalem, Alexandria and Antioch were reduced to Constantinople, the empire's capital, and the Frankish state, centered away from the [Islamic domination of the Mediterranean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests), evolved into the dominant power that shaped the Western Europe of the Middle Ages.[^cite_note-81] Battles in [Toulouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Toulouse_\(721\)) and [Tours](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Tours) halted Islamic advancements in the West; a failed siege of [Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Constantinople_\(717%E2%80%93718\)) halted them in the East. In 751, the Byzantine Empire lost the city of [Ravenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ravenna), which [governed the small fragments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exarchate_of_Ravenna) of Italy, including Rome, to the Lombards, meaning confirmation by a no longer existent [exarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exarch) was not asked during the election of [Pope Stephen II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Stephen_II) in 752—the papacy had to look elsewhere for a civil power to protect it.[^cite_note-82] The Frankish king [Pepin the Short](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pepin_the_Short) conquered the Lombards in 754 at the urgent request of Pope Stephen, and then gifted the lands back to the pope, initiating the time of the [Papal States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_States). In the 860s, Rome and the Byzantine East had a conflict during the [Photian schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photian_schism), as [Photius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photios_I_of_Constantinople) criticized the [Latin West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_West) for adding the *[filioque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filioque)* clause, after being [excommunicated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excommunication) by [Nicholas I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Nicholas_I), causing unresolved issues that led to further divisions.[^cite_note-83]



A painting of St. [Catherine of Siena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_of_Siena) by [Carlo Dolci](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlo_Dolci). A 14th century Catholic mystic associated with [Dominican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Preachers) spirituality, she helped to heal the [Great Western Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Western_Schism).

In the 11th century the efforts of [Hildebrand of Sovana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hildebrand_of_Sovana) led to the creation of the [College of Cardinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Cardinals) to elect new popes, starting with [Pope Alexander II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Alexander_II) in the [papal election of 1061](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1061_papal_election). When Alexander II died, Hildebrand was elected to succeed him, as [Pope Gregory VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Gregory_VII). The basic election system of the College of Cardinals which Gregory VII helped establish has continued to function into the 21st century. Pope Gregory VII further initiated the [Gregorian Reforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregorian_Reforms) regarding the independence of the clergy from secular authority. This led to the [Investiture Controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Investiture_Controversy) between the church and the [Holy Roman emperors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor), over which had the authority to appoint bishops and popes.[^cite_note-Vidmar107-84][^cite_note-Duffy78-85]

In 1095 the Byzantine emperor, [Alexius I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexius_I), appealed to [Pope Urban II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Urban_II) for help against renewed Muslim invasions in the [Byzantine–Seljuk wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine%E2%80%93Seljuk_wars),[^cite_note-rileysmith-86] which caused Urban to launch the [First Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Crusade) aimed at aiding the Byzantine Empire and returning the [Holy Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Land) to Christian control.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004140–41-87] In the [11th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_the_11th_century) strained relations between the primarily Greek church and the Latin Church separated them in the East–West Schism, partially due to conflicts over papal authority. The [Fourth Crusade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fourth_Crusade) and the sacking of Constantinople by renegade crusaders proved the final breach.[^cite_note-88]

In the twelfth century, [inquisitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inquisitions) began in the Catholic [Kingdom of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_France) in response to the [Albigensians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albigensian). The system spread throughout other European countries in the succeeding centuries, through multiple forms: first as individual inquisitors sporadically appointed for certain problem areas by popes, then as state-sponsored tribunals.[^cite_note-89] The *ad hoc* use of torture by secular medieval judges was common, and the directives governing inquisitions progressively allowed various situations where non-maiming, non-bloody torture could or must be used to corroborate testimony, not only on some classes of accused but sometimes even on denouncers and witnesses.[^cite_note-90]

In the early 13th century [mendicant orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mendicant_orders) were founded by [Francis of Assisi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_of_Assisi) and [Dominic de Guzmán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominic_de_Guzm%C3%A1n). The *studia conventualia* and *[studia generalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Studia_generalia)* of the mendicant orders played a large role in the transformation of church-sponsored cathedral schools and palace schools, such as that of [Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne) at [Aachen](1520.md), into the prominent universities of Europe.[^cite_note-91] [Scholastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism) theologians and philosophers such as the Dominican priest [Thomas Aquinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas) studied and taught at these studia. Aquinas' *[Summa Theologica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summa_Theologica)* was an intellectual milestone in its synthesis of the legacy of [ancient Greek philosophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_philosophers) such as [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato) and [Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle) with the content of Christian revelation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004158–59-92]

A growing sense of church-state conflicts marked the 14th century. In 1309, to escape instability in Rome, [Pope Clement V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Clement_V) became the first of seven popes to reside in the fortified city of [Avignon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avignon) in southern France[^cite_note-Duffy122-93] during a period known as the [Avignon Papacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avignon_Papacy). The Avignon Papacy ended in 1376 when the pope returned to Rome.[^cite_note-McManners232-94] In 1378 a 38-year-long [Western Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Schism) began, with claimants to the papacy located in Rome, Avignon and, after 1409, Pisa.[^cite_note-McManners232-94] The matter was largely resolved in 1414–1418 at the [Council of Constance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Constance), with the claimants in Rome and Pisa agreeing to resign and the third claimant [excommunicated](28394423.md) by the cardinals, who held a new election naming [Martin V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_V) pope.[^cite_note-McManners240-95]

In 1438 the [Council of Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Florence) convened, which featured a strong dialogue focused on understanding the theological differences between the East and West, with the hope of reuniting the Catholic and Orthodox churches.[^cite_note-96] Several eastern churches reunited, forming the majority of the [Eastern Catholic Churches](214903.md).[^cite_note-97]

### Age of Discovery and Counter-Reformation

Main articles: [Catholic Church and the Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_the_Age_of_Discovery) and [Counter-Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Reformation)





With the rise of Protestantism, the Catholic Church lost some adherents in Europe. Counter-Reformation groups such as the [Jesuits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesuits) were founded to tackle this. At the same time, Catholicism spread in the Americas through evangelisation, represented by the apparition of [Our Lady of Guadalupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Guadalupe).

The [Age of Discovery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Discovery) beginning in the 15th century saw the expansion of Western Europe's political and cultural influence worldwide. Because of the rise in power overseas of strongly Catholic nations of Spain and Portugal (as well as France), Catholicism was spread to the Americas, Asia and Oceania by explorers, conquistadors, and missionaries, as well as by the conversion of people who lived in these societies to the Catholic faith. [Pope Alexander VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Alexander_VI) had awarded sovereignty rights over most of the newly discovered lands to [Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) and [Portugal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_Empire) (later confirmed by the [Treaty of Tordesillas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Tordesillas))[^cite_note-Koschorke13-98] and the ensuing *[patronato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patronato_real)* system allowed state authorities, not the Vatican, to control all clerical appointments in the new colonies.[^cite_note-99] In 1521 the Portuguese explorer [Ferdinand Magellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Magellan) made the first Catholic converts in the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies).[^cite_note-Koschorke21-100] Elsewhere, Portuguese missionaries under the Spanish Jesuit [Francis Xavier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_Xavier) evangelized in India, China and Japan.[^cite_note-Koschorke3-101] The [French colonization of the Americas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_colonization_of_the_Americas) beginning in the 16th century established a Catholic [Francophone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) population and forbade non-Catholics to settle in [Quebec](7954867.md).[^cite_note-102]

In 1415 popular Bohemian preacher [Jan Hus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jan_Hus) was burned at the stake for refusing to recant [Wycliffite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wycliffite) heresies.[^cite_note-103] His "hot-headed" reform efforts presaged [Martin Luther](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther), an [Augustinian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Order_of_Saint_Augustine) friar in Germany, who [sent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Lutheranism#The_start_of_the_Reformation) a list of topics for academic disputation, the *[Ninety-five Theses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ninety-five_Theses)*, to several bishops in 1517.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004215-104] His theses protested against some Catholic [doctrines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doctrine) as well as contemporary practices such as the supposed sale of [indulgences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indulgence), and these were the start of a rapidly escalating series of inflammatory works ending with *[On the Babylonian Captivity of the Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Babylonian_Captivity_of_the_Church)* (1520) which accused the Pope of being the anti-Christ:[^cite_note-105] this led to [his excommunication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Luther#Excommunication) in 1521.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004215-104][^cite_note-Vidmar184-106] In Switzerland [Huldrych Zwingli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huldrych_Zwingli), [John Calvin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Calvin) and other [Protestant Reformers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Reformers) further criticized certain Catholic teachings. These challenges developed into the Reformation, which gave birth to the great majority of [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant) [denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Christian_denominations)[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004223–24-107] and also [crypto-Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crypto-Protestantism) within the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-108] Meanwhile, [Henry VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_VIII) of the [Kingdom of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_England) petitioned [Pope Clement VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Clement_VII) for a [declaration of nullity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_nullity) concerning his marriage to [Catherine of Aragon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catherine_of_Aragon). When this was denied, he had the [Acts of Supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acts_of_Supremacy) passed to make himself [Supreme Head](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Head) of the [Church of England](5955.md), spurring the [English Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_Reformation) and the eventual development of [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004235–37-109]



Ruins of the [Jesuit mission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesuit_missions_among_the_Guaran%C3%AD) of [São Miguel das Missões](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruins_of_S%C3%A3o_Miguel_das_Miss%C3%B5es) in Brazil

The Reformation contributed to clashes between the Protestant [Schmalkaldic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schmalkaldic_League) and the Catholic Emperor [Charles V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_V,_Holy_Roman_Emperor) and his allies. The first nine-year war ended in 1555 with the [Peace of Augsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Augsburg) but continued tensions produced a far graver conflict—the [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years'_War)—which broke out in 1618.[^cite_note-Vidmar233-110] In France a series of conflicts termed the [French Wars of Religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Wars_of_Religion) was fought from 1562 to 1598 between the [Huguenots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huguenot) (French [Calvinists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calvinists)) and the forces of the [French Catholic League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Catholic_League), which were backed and funded by a series of popes.[^cite_note-Duffy177-111] This ended under [Pope Clement VIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Clement_VIII), who hesitantly accepted King [Henry IV of France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_IV_of_France)'s 1598 [Edict of Nantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edict_of_Nantes) granting civil and [religious toleration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_toleration) to French Protestants.[^cite_note-Vidmar233-110][^cite_note-Duffy177-111]

The [Council of Trent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Trent) (1545–1563) became the driving force behind the [Counter-Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-Reformation) in response to the Protestant movement. Doctrinally, it reaffirmed many central Catholic teachings such as [transubstantiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transubstantiation), the keeping of the [sacraments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacraments_of_the_Catholic_Church), and the requirement of [good works](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_works#Catholic_Church) anchored in love and hope to justify one's salvation, as well as faith as a necessary condition to attain such salvation.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004242–44-112] In subsequent centuries, Catholicism spread widely across the world, in part through missionaries and [imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperialism), although its hold on European populations declined due to the growth of [religious skepticism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_skepticism) during and after the Enlightenment.[^cite_note-113]

From the 17th century onward, the Enlightenment questioned the power and influence of the Catholic Church over Western society.[^cite_note-Pollard8-114] In the 18th century, writers such as [Voltaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voltaire) and the *[Encyclopédistes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclop%C3%A9distes)* wrote biting critiques of both religion and the Catholic Church. One target of their criticism was the 1685 [revocation of the Edict of Nantes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revocation_of_the_Edict_of_Nantes) by King [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV) of France, which ended a century-long policy of religious toleration of Protestant Huguenots. As the papacy resisted pushes for [Gallicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallicanism), the [French Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution) in 1789 shifted power to the state, caused the destruction of churches, the establishment of a [Cult of Reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cult_of_Reason),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter2004283–85-115] and the martyrdom of [nuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martyrs_of_Compi%C3%A8gne) during the [Reign of Terror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reign_of_Terror).[^cite_note-116] In 1798 [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon)'s General [Louis-Alexandre Berthier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis-Alexandre_Berthier) invaded the [Italian Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Peninsula), imprisoning [Pope Pius VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Pius_VI), who died in captivity. Napoleon later re-established the Catholic Church in France through the [Concordat of 1801](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Concordat_of_1801).[^cite_note-Collins176-117] The end of the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars) brought Catholic revival and the return of the [Papal States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_States).[^cite_note-118]

In 1854 [Pope Pius IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX), with the support of the overwhelming majority of Catholic bishops, whom he had consulted from 1851 to 1853, proclaimed the [Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immaculate_Conception) as a [dogma in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogma_in_the_Catholic_Church).[^cite_note-119] In 1870 the [First Vatican Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Vatican_Council) affirmed the doctrine of [papal infallibility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_infallibility) when exercised in specifically defined pronouncements,[^cite_note-Leith-120][^cite_note-Duffy232-121] striking a blow to the rival position of [conciliarism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conciliar_Church). Controversy over this and other issues resulted in a breakaway movement called the [Old Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Catholic_Church#First_Vatican_Council,_Old_Catholic_Union_of_Utrecht).[^cite_note-Fahlbusch-122]

The [Italian unification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_unification) of the 1860s incorporated the Papal States, including Rome itself from 1870, into the [Kingdom of Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Italy), thus ending the papacy's [temporal power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temporal_power_\(papal\)). In response Pius IX excommunicated King [Victor Emmanuel II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Emmanuel_II), refused payment for the land and rejected the Italian [Law of Guarantees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_of_Guarantees), which granted him special privileges. To avoid placing himself in visible subjection to the Italian authorities, he remained a "[prisoner in the Vatican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prisoner_in_the_Vatican)".[^cite_note-123] This stand-off, which was spoken of as the [Roman question](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_question), was resolved by the [Lateran Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lateran_Treaty) in 1929, whereby the Holy See acknowledged Italian sovereignty over the former [Papal States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_States) in return for payment and Italy's recognition of papal sovereignty over Vatican City as a new sovereign and independent state.[^cite_note-124]

Catholic missionaries generally supported, and sought to facilitate, the European imperial powers' [conquest of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conquest_of_Africa) during the late nineteenth century. According to the historian of religion [Adrian Hastings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrian_Hastings), Catholic missionaries were generally unwilling to defend African rights or encourage Africans to see themselves as equals to Europeans, in contrast to Protestant missionaries, who were more willing to oppose colonial injustices.[^cite_note-125]

### 20th century

Main article: [Catholic Church in the 20th century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_20th_century)



Members of the Canadian Army's [Royal 22nd Regiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_22nd_Regiment) in audience with [Pope Pius XII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII) on 4 July 1944, following the [Battle of Anzio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Anzio), which liberated Rome from [Nazi German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_German) and the [Italian fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_Social_Republic) occupation during [World War II](32927.md).



Bishops listen during the [Second Vatican Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council) in the early 1960s



[Pope John Paul II](23805.md) and then U.S. president [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) (pictured with his wife [Nancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Reagan)) meeting in June 1982; both Pope John Paul II and Reagan were credited with contributing to the [Revolutions of 1989](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1989), which led to the fall of communism and the end of the [Cold War](325329.md) two years later, in 1991.

During the 20th century, the church's global reach continued to grow, despite the rise of [anti-Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Catholic) authoritarian regimes and the collapse of European [colonial empires](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_empire), accompanied by a general decline in religious observance in the West. Under the popes [Benedict XV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benedict_XV) and [Pius XII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pius_XII) the Holy See sought to maintain public neutrality through the World Wars, acting as peace broker and delivering aid to the victims of the conflicts. In the 1960s [Pope John XXIII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_John_XXIII) convened the [Second Vatican Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council), which ushered in radical change to church ritual and practice, and in the later 20th century the long papacy of [Pope John Paul II](23805.md) contributed to the [fall of communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fall_of_communism) in Europe, and a new public and international role for the papacy.[^cite_note-126][^cite_note-communist-127] From the late 20th century, the Catholic Church has been [criticized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_the_Catholic_Church) for its doctrines on [sexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_teachings_on_sexual_morality), its inability to [ordain women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_doctrine_on_the_ordination_of_women) and its handling of [sexual abuse cases](158934.md).

The Second Vatican Council (1962–1965) introduced the most significant changes to Catholic practices since the Council of Trent, four centuries before.[^cite_note-128] Initiated by Pope John XXIII, this ecumenical council modernized the practices of the Catholic Church, allowing the Mass to be said in the [vernacular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernacular) (local language) and encouraging "fully conscious, and active participation in liturgical celebrations".[^cite_note-129] It intended to engage the church more closely with the present world (*[aggiornamento](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aggiornamento)*), which was described by its advocates as an "opening of the windows".[^cite_note-Duffy272-130] In addition to changes in the liturgy, it led to changes to the church's approach to [ecumenism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_ecumenism),[^cite_note-131] and a call to improved relations with non-Christian religions, especially Judaism, in its document *[Nostra aetate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nostra_aetate)*.[^cite_note-NOSTRA_AETATE-132]

The council, however, generated significant controversy in implementing its reforms: proponents of the "[Spirit of Vatican II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirit_of_Vatican_II)" such as the Swiss theologian [Hans Küng](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_K%C3%BCng) said that Vatican II had "not gone far enough" to change church policies.[^cite_note-133] [Traditionalist Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Traditionalist_Catholics), such as [Archbishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archbishop) [Marcel Lefebvre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_Lefebvre), however, strongly criticized the council, arguing that its liturgical reforms led "to the destruction of the Holy Sacrifice of the Mass and the sacraments", among other issues.[^cite_note-134] The teaching on the morality of [contraception](18978770.md) also came under scrutiny; after a series of disagreements, *[Humanae vitae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanae_vitae)* upheld the church's prohibition of all forms of contraception.[^cite_note-135][^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137][^cite_note-HV_then_now-138]

In 1978 [Pope John Paul II](23805.md), formerly [Archbishop of Kraków](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archbishop_of_Krak%C3%B3w) in the [Polish People's Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_People's_Republic), became the first non-Italian pope in 455 years. His 26 1/2-year [pontificate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontificate) was one of the longest in history and was credited with hastening the fall of communism in Europe.[^cite_note-139][^cite_note-140] John Paul II sought to evangelize an increasingly [secular world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism). He traveled more than any other pope, visiting 129 countries,[^cite_note-141] and used television and radio as means of spreading the church's teachings. He also emphasized the [dignity of work](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dignity_of_work) and natural rights of labourers to have [fair wages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Living_wage) and safe conditions in *[Laborem exercens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laborem_exercens)*.[^cite_note-142] He emphasized several church teachings, including moral exhortations [against abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_abortion), [euthanasia](9587.md) and [against the widespread use of capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_capital_punishment), in *[Evangelium Vitae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelium_Vitae)*.[^cite_note-143]

### 21st century

[Pope Benedict XVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XVI), elected in 2005, was known for upholding traditional [Christian values](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_values) against [secularization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularization),[^cite_note-144] and for increasing use of the [Tridentine Mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tridentine_Mass) as found in the [Roman Missal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Missal) of 1962, which he titled the "Extraordinary Form".[^cite_note-145] Citing the frailties of advanced age, [Benedict resigned](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resignation_of_Pope_Benedict_XVI) in 2013, becoming the first pope to do so in nearly 600 years.[^cite_note-146]

[Pope Francis](1687680.md) became in 2013 the first pope from the Americas, the first from the [Southern Hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Hemisphere) and the first from outside Europe since the eighth-century [Gregory III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Gregory_III).[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] Francis made efforts to further close Catholicism's estrangement with the Eastern churches.[^cite_note-orthodox-149] His installation was attended by [Patriarch Bartholomew I of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Bartholomew_I_of_Constantinople) of the Eastern Orthodox Church,[^cite_note-patriarch-150] the first time since the [Great Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) of 1054 that the [Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_Patriarch_of_Constantinople) has attended a papal installation,[^cite_note-151] while he also met [Patriarch Kirill of Moscow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patriarch_Kirill_of_Moscow), head of the largest Eastern Orthodox church, in 2016; this was the first such high-level meeting between the two churches since the Great Schism of 1054.[^cite_note-152] In 2017 during a visit in [Egypt](8087628.md), Pope Francis re-established mutual recognition of baptism with the [Coptic Orthodox Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church).[^cite_note-153]

[Pope Leo XIV](54520372.md) was elected as Pope in the [2025 conclave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_conclave), following the death of Francis. He is the first Augustinian pope, the first North American pope (born in [Chicago](6886.md) in the [United States](3434750.md)), and the first pope of [Peruvian](170691.md) citizenship.[^cite_note-Atlantic2025-154]

## Organization

Main articles: [Hierarchy of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchy_of_the_Catholic_Church) and [Catholic Church by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_by_country)



The crossed keys of the Holy See symbolize those of [Simon Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Peter). The triple crown [papal tiara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_tiara) symbolizes the triple power of the pope as "father of kings", "governor of the world" and "[Vicar of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vicar_of_Christ)". The gold cross symbolizes the sovereignty of Jesus.

The Catholic Church follows an [episcopal polity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_polity), led by bishops who have received the sacrament of [holy orders](#Sacraments_at_the_service_of_communion) who are given formal [jurisdictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_jurisdiction) of governance within the church.[^cite_note-CCC880-155][^cite_note-CEHierarchy-156] There are three levels of clergy: the episcopate, composed of bishops who hold jurisdiction over a geographic area called a [diocese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese) or [eparchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eparchy); the presbyterate, composed of priests ordained by bishops and who work in local dioceses or religious orders; and the diaconate, composed of deacons who assist bishops and priests in a variety of ministerial roles. Ultimately leading the entire Catholic Church is the [bishop of Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bishop_of_Rome), known as the pope ([Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *papa*, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'father'), whose jurisdiction is called the [Holy See](13393.md) (*Sancta Sedes* in Latin).[^cite_note-section880-157]

In parallel to the diocesan structure are a variety of [religious institutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_institute) that function autonomously, often subject only to the authority of the pope, though sometimes subject to the local bishop. Most religious institutes only have male or female members but some have both. Additionally, [lay members](31488305.md) aid many liturgical functions during worship services. The Catholic Church has been described as the oldest [multinational organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multinational_organization) in the world.[^cite_note-158][^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160]

### Holy See, papacy, Roman Curia, and College of Cardinals

Main articles: [Holy See](13393.md), [Pope](23056.md), [Roman Curia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Curia), and [College of Cardinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Cardinals)

Further information: [List of popes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_popes)



[Pope Leo XIV](54520372.md), the [267th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_popes) and current pope of the Catholic Church, a [title](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Courtesy_title) he holds *[ex officio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ex_officio)* as bishop of Rome and sovereign of Vatican City, was elected in the [2025 papal conclave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_papal_conclave).

The [hierarchy of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchy_of_the_Catholic_Church) is headed[^cite_note-166] by the pope, currently [Pope Leo XIV](54520372.md), who was elected on 8 May 2025 by [a papal conclave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_papal_conclave). The office of the pope is known as the *papacy*. The Catholic Church holds that Christ instituted the papacy upon giving the [keys of Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keys_of_Heaven) to [Saint Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Peter). His ecclesiastical jurisdiction is called the Holy See, or the [Apostolic See](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_See) (meaning the see of the apostle Peter).[^cite_note-167][^cite_note-168] Directly serving the pope is the Roman Curia, the central governing body that administers the day-to-day business of the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-169]

The pope is also [sovereign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sovereign) of Vatican City,[^cite_note-170] a small [city-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state) entirely enclaved within the city of Rome, which is an entity distinct from the Holy See. It is as head of the Holy See, not as head of Vatican City State, that the pope receives ambassadors of states and sends them his own diplomatic representatives.[^cite_note-171]

The position of [cardinal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardinal_\(Catholicism\)) is a rank of honour bestowed by popes on certain clerics, such as leaders within the Roman Curia, bishops serving in major cities and distinguished theologians. For advice and assistance in governing, the pope may turn to the [College of Cardinals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Cardinals).[^cite_note-McDonough227-172]

Following the death or resignation of a pope,[^cite_note-174] members of the College of Cardinals who are under age 80 act as an [electoral college](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electoral_college), meeting in a [papal conclave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_conclave) to elect a successor.[^cite_note-duffy416-175] Although the conclave may elect any male Catholic in the world as pope, since 1389 only cardinals have been elected.[^cite_note-duffy417and18-176]

Catholic canon law ([Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *jus canonicum*)[^cite_note-177] is the system of laws and [legal principles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law) made and enforced by the [hierarchical authorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hierarchy_of_the_Catholic_Church) of the Catholic Church to regulate its external organization and government and to order and direct the activities of Catholics toward the mission of the church.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDella_Rocca19593-178] The canon law of the Latin Church was the first modern Western [legal system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_system),[^cite_note-179] and is the oldest continuously functioning legal system in the West,[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181] while the distinctive traditions of [Eastern Catholic canon law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_canon_law) govern the 23 Eastern Catholic [particular churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular_church) *[sui iuris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sui_iuris)*.[^cite_note-182]

Positive ecclesiastical laws, based directly or indirectly upon immutable divine law or [natural law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_law), derive formal authority in the case of universal laws from [promulgation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Promulgation_\(Catholic_canon_law\)) by the supreme legislator—the [Supreme Pontiff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Pontiff)—who possesses the totality of legislative, executive and judicial power in his person,[^cite_note-183] while particular laws derive formal authority from promulgation by a legislator inferior to the supreme legislator, whether an ordinary or a delegated legislator. The actual subject material of the canons is not just doctrinal or moral in nature, but all-encompassing of the human condition. It has all the ordinary elements of a mature legal system:[^cite_note-clinfocat-184] laws, [courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_court), lawyers, judges,[^cite_note-clinfocat-184] a fully articulated [legal code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Code_of_Canon_Law) for the Latin Church[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDella_Rocca195949-185] as well as a [code](3547385.md) for the Eastern Catholic Churches,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEDella_Rocca195949-185] principles of [legal interpretation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interpretation_\(Catholic_canon_law\)),[^cite_note-186] and coercive penalties.[^cite_note-187][^cite_note-188]

[Canon law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law_of_the_Catholic_Church) concerns the Catholic Church's life and organization and is distinct from civil law. In its own field it gives force to civil law only by specific enactment in matters such as the guardianship of minors.[^cite_note-189] Similarly, civil law may give force in its field to canon law, but only by specific enactment, as with regard to canonical marriages.[^cite_note-Malta-190] Currently, the [1983 Code of Canon Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Code_of_Canon_Law) is in effect for the Latin Church.[^cite_note-191] The distinct 1990 *[Code of Canons of the Eastern Churches](3547385.md)* (*CCEO*, after the Latin initials) applies to the autonomous Eastern Catholic Churches.[^cite_note-192]

In the first thousand years of Catholic history, different varieties of Christianity developed in the Western and [Eastern Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christian) areas of Europe, Asia and Africa. Though most Eastern-tradition churches are no longer in communion with the Catholic Church after the [Great Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) of 1054 (as well as the earlier [Nestorian Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestorian_Schism) and [Chalcedonian Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcedonian_Schism)), 23 autonomous [particular churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular_churches) of eastern traditions participate in the Catholic communion, also known as "churches *[sui iuris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sui_iuris)*" ([Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_language): *"of one's own right*"). The largest and most well known is the Latin Church, the only Western-tradition church, with more than 1 billion members worldwide. Relatively small in terms of adherents compared to the Latin Church, are the 23 self-governing Eastern Catholic Churches with a combined membership of 17.3 million as of 2010.[^cite_note-Roberson-193][^cite_note-Gunton-194][^cite_note-195][^cite_note-196]

The Latin Church is governed by the pope and diocesan bishops directly appointed by him. The pope exercises a direct [patriarchal](175409.md) role over the Latin Church, which is considered to form the original and still major part of [Western Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Christianity), a heritage of certain beliefs and customs originating in Europe and northwestern Africa, some of which are inherited by many [Christian denominations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_denominations) that trace their origins to the Protestant Reformation.[^cite_note-west_christ-197]

The Eastern Catholic Churches follow the traditions and spirituality of [Eastern Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christianity) and are churches that have always remained in full communion with the Catholic Church or who have chosen to re-enter full communion in the centuries following the [East–West Schism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Schism) or earlier divisions. These churches are communities of Catholic Christians whose forms of worship reflect distinct historical and cultural influences rather than differences in doctrine.[^cite_note-Blackwell_Publishing-198]

The pope's recognition of Eastern Catholic Churches has caused controversy in [ecumenical relations with the Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic%E2%80%93Eastern_Orthodox_relations) and other eastern churches. Historically, pressure to conform to the norms of the Western Christianity practised by the majority Latin Church led to a degree of encroachment ([Liturgical Latinisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_Latinisation)) on some of the Eastern Catholic traditions. The [Second Vatican Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council) document, *[Orientalium Ecclesiarum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orientalium_Ecclesiarum)*, built on previous reforms to reaffirm the right of Eastern Catholics to maintain their distinct liturgical practices.[^cite_note-Blackwell_Publishing-198]

A church *sui iuris* is defined in the *[Code of Canons for the Eastern Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Code_of_Canons_for_the_Eastern_Churches)* as a "group of Christian faithful united by a hierarchy" that is recognized by the pope in his capacity as the [supreme authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_supremacy) on matters of doctrine within the church.[^cite_note-199] The Eastern Catholic Churches are in [full communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_communion) with the pope, but have governance structures and liturgical traditions separate from that of the Latin Church.[^cite_note-Gunton-194]

Some Eastern Catholic churches are governed by a patriarch who is elected by the [synod](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synod) of the bishops of that church,[^cite_note-CCEO55_150-200] others are headed by a [major archbishop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_archbishop),[^cite_note-CCEO151_154-201] others are under a [metropolitan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metropolitan_bishop),[^cite_note-CCEO155_173-202] and others are organized as individual [eparchies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eparchies).[^cite_note-CCEO-203] Each church has authority over the particulars of its internal organization, [liturgical rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_liturgical_rites), [liturgical calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar) and other aspects of its spirituality, subject only to the authority of the pope.[^cite_note-CCEO27_7-204] The Roman Curia has a specific department, the [Congregation for the Oriental Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Oriental_Churches), to maintain relations with them.[^cite_note-205]

### Dioceses, parishes, organizations, and institutes

Further information: [List of Catholic dioceses (structured view)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Catholic_dioceses_\(structured_view\)), [Parish in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parish_in_the_Catholic_Church), [Religious institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_institute), and [Catholic charities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_charities)

Distribution of Catholics

[^cite_note-206]



The percentage of Catholics by country, 2010



The number of Catholics by country, 2010\
&#x20;

- [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:Catholicism_maps)

- [t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:Catholicism_maps?action=edit\&redlink=1)

- [e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:Catholicism_maps)

Individual countries, regions, and major cities are served by [particular churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular_church) known as [dioceses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diocese) in the [Latin Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Church), or [eparchies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eparchies) in the [Eastern Catholic Churches](214903.md), each of which are overseen by a bishop. As of 2021, the Catholic Church [has 3,171 dioceses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Catholic_dioceses_\(structured_view\)) globally.[^cite_note-sees-207] The bishops in a particular country are members of a national or regional episcopal conference.[^cite_note-208]

Dioceses are divided into parishes, each with one or more [priests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Priesthood_\(Catholic_Church\)), [deacons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deacon), or [lay ecclesial ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lay_ecclesial_ministers).[^cite_note-OneFaith52-209] Parishes are responsible for the day to day celebration of the sacraments and pastoral care of the laity.[^cite_note-210] As of 2016 there are approximately 221,700 parishes worldwide.[^cite_note-Vermont_winter-7]

In the Latin Church, Catholic men may serve as deacons or priests by receiving sacramental [ordination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Orders). Men and women may serve as [extraordinary ministers of Holy Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extraordinary_ministers_of_Holy_Communion), as readers ([lectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reader_\(liturgy\))), or as [altar servers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altar_servers). Historically, boys and men have only been permitted to serve as altar servers; however, since the 1990s, girls and women have also been permitted.[^cite_note-Apostalicae86-211][^cite_note-212]

Catholics may enter into [consecrated life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecrated_life) either on an individual basis, as a [hermit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermit) or [consecrated virgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecrated_virgin), or by joining an [institute of consecrated life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_consecrated_life) (a religious institute or a [secular institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular_institute)) in which to take [vows](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_vows) confirming their desire to follow the three [evangelical counsels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelical_counsels) of [chastity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chastity), poverty and obedience.[^cite_note-Canons573-746-213] Examples of institutes of consecrated life are the [Benedictines](4240.md), the [Carmelites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmelites), the [Dominicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominican_Order), the [Franciscans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franciscan), the [Missionaries of Charity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionaries_of_Charity), the [Legionaries of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legionaries_of_Christ) and the [Sisters of Mercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisters_of_Mercy).[^cite_note-Canons573-746-213]

"Religious institutes" is a modern term encompassing both "[religious orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_order_\(Catholic\))" and "[religious congregations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_congregations)", which were once distinguished in [canon law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law).[^cite_note-214] The terms "religious order" and "religious institute" tend to be used as synonyms colloquially.[^cite_note-215]

By means of [Catholic charities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_charities) and beyond, the Catholic Church is the largest non-government provider of [education](1452366.md) and [health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_health_care) in the world.[^cite_note-Geopolitics-216]
