As of 2020 Catholicism is the second-largest [religious body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_denomination) in the world after [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam).[^cite_note-217] Catholics represent about half of all Christians.[^cite_note-218] According to the *[World Christian Database](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Christian_Database)*, there are 1.279 billion Catholics constituting 47.8% of 2.674 billion Christians globally, as of 2026.[^cite_note-WorldChristianDatabase-4] According to the *[Annuario Pontificio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annuario_Pontificio)*, church membership, defined as baptized Catholics, was 1.406 billion at the end of 2023, which was 17.4% of the world population:[^cite_note-Yearbook_3/20/2025-5] Under Pope Francis the church membership grew by almost 11%, with growth concentrated in Africa and loss in Europe.[^cite_note-219]

Brazil has the largest Catholic population in the world, followed by Mexico, the Philippines and the United States.[^cite_note-220]

Geographic distribution of Catholics worldwide continues to shift, with 20.0% in [Africa](5334607.md), 47.8% in the [Americas](29833.md), 11.0% in [Asia](689.md), 20.4% in [Europe](9239.md) and 0.8% in [Oceania](22621.md).[^cite_note-Yearbook_3/20/2025-5]

Catholic ministers include ordained clergy, [lay ecclesial ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lay_ecclesial_ministers), [missionaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missionaries) and [catechists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechists). Also as of the end of 2023, there were 463,859 ordained clergy, including 5,430 bishops, 406,996 priests (diocesan and religious) and 51,433 deacons (permanent).[^cite_note-Yearbook_3/20/2025-5] Non-ordained ministers, as at October 2024, include 2,883,049 catechists and 413,561 lay missionaries.[^cite_note-221]

Catholics who have committed to religious or consecrated life as a state of life or relational vocation include 49,414 male religious (as of 2022) and 589,423 women religious (as of 2023). These are not ordained, nor generally called "ministers" unless also engaged in one of the lay minister categories above.[^cite_note-Yearbook_3/20/2025-5]

## Doctrine

Main article: [Catholic theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_theology)

Catholic doctrine has developed over the centuries, reflecting direct teachings of early Christians, formal definitions of [heretical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heresy_in_Christianity) and [orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthodoxy) beliefs by [ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_councils) and in [papal bulls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_bull), and theological debate by scholars. The church believes that it is continually guided by the Holy Spirit as it discerns new theological issues and is protected [infallibly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infallibility_of_the_Church) from falling into doctrinal error when a firm decision on an issue is reached.[^cite_note-222][^cite_note-223]

It teaches that revelation has one common source, [God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_in_Christianity), and two distinct modes of transmission: Sacred Scripture and [Sacred Tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_Tradition),[^cite_note-224][^cite_note-LumenG3-225] and that these are authentically interpreted by the [Magisterium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magisterium).[^cite_note-226][^cite_note-227] Sacred Scripture consists of the 73 books of the [Catholic Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Bible), consisting of 46 [Old Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Testament) and 27 [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) writings. Sacred Tradition consists of those teachings believed by the church to have been handed down since the time of the Apostles.[^cite_note-Schreck16-228] Sacred Scripture and Sacred Tradition are collectively known as the "deposit of faith" (*depositum fidei* in Latin). These are in turn interpreted by the Magisterium (from *magister*, Latin for "teacher"), the church's teaching authority, which is exercised by the pope and the [College of Bishops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/College_of_Bishops) in union with the pope.[^cite_note-Schreck30-229] Catholic doctrine is authoritatively summarized in the *[Catechism of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church)*, published by the Holy See.[^cite_note-cat-230][^cite_note-231]

### Nature of God

Main article: [Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinity)



This diagram depicts the [Shield of the Trinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shield_of_the_Trinity), which expresses many aspects of the doctrine of the Trinity.

The Catholic Church holds that there is one [eternal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attributes_of_God_in_Christianity#Eternity) God, who exists as a *[perichoresis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perichoresis)* ("mutual indwelling") of three *[hypostases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypostasis_\(philosophy_and_religion\))*, or "persons": [God the Father](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Father#Christianity); [God the Son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Son); and [God the Holy Spirit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/God_the_Holy_Spirit) (also called the Holy Ghost), which together are called the "Holy Trinity".[^cite_note-232_252-232]

Catholics believe that Jesus Christ is the "Second Person" of the Trinity, God the Son. In an event known as the [Incarnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incarnation_\(Christianity\)), through the power of the Holy Spirit, God became united with human nature through the conception of Christ in the womb of the [Blessed Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church). Christ, therefore, is understood as being both fully divine and fully human, including possessing a human [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul). It is taught that Christ's mission on earth included giving people his teachings and providing his example for them to follow as recorded in the four [Gospels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel).[^cite_note-McGrath-233] Jesus is believed to have remained sinless while on earth, and to have allowed himself to be unjustly executed by [crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus), as a sacrifice of himself to reconcile humanity to God; this reconciliation is known as the [Paschal Mystery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paschal_Mystery).[^cite_note-234] The Greek term "Christ" and the Hebrew "Messiah" both mean "anointed one", referring to the Christian belief that Jesus' death and resurrection are the fulfillment of the Old Testament's [messianic prophecies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesus_and_messianic_prophecy).[^cite_note-Kreeft71-235]

The Catholic Church teaches dogmatically that "the Holy Spirit proceeds eternally from the Father and the Son, not as from two principles but as from one single principle".[^cite_note-236] It holds that the Father, as the "principle without principle", is the first origin of the Spirit, but also that he, as Father of the only Son, is with the Son the single principle from which the Spirit proceeds.[^cite_note-237] This belief is expressed in the *[Filioque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filioque)* clause which was added to the Latin version of the Nicene Creed of 381 but not included in the Greek versions of the creed used in Eastern Christianity.[^cite_note-245_248-238]

### Nature of the church

Main article: [Catholic ecclesiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_ecclesiology)

The Catholic Church teaches that it is the "[one true church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_true_church)",[^cite_note-Catholic_News_Service-9][^cite_note-note1cite1-11] "the universal sacrament of salvation for the human race",[^cite_note-GAUDIUM-239][^cite_note-ReferenceC-240] and "the one true religion".[^cite_note-241] According to the *Catechism*, the Catholic Church is further described in the Nicene Creed as the "one, holy, catholic, and apostolic Church".[^cite_note-242] These are collectively known as the [Four Marks of the Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Marks_of_the_Church). The church teaches that its founder is Jesus Christ.[^cite_note-Kreeft98-243][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter200430-39] The [New Testament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Testament) records several events considered integral to the establishment of the Catholic Church, including Jesus' activities and teaching and his appointment of the [apostles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostles_in_the_New_Testament) as witnesses to his ministry, suffering, and resurrection. The [Great Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Commission), after his resurrection, instructed the apostles to continue his work. The coming of the Holy Spirit upon the apostles, in an event known as [Pentecost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecost), is seen as the beginning of the public ministry of the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-Barry48-42] The church teaches that all duly consecrated bishops have a lineal succession from the apostles of Christ, known as [apostolic succession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_succession).[^cite_note-Barry48-42] In particular, the Bishop of Rome (the pope) is considered the successor to the apostle [Simon Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Peter), a position from which he derives his [supremacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papal_supremacy) over the church.[^cite_note-244]

Catholic belief holds that the church "is the continuing presence of Jesus on earth"[^cite_note-Schreck131-245] and that it alone possesses the full means of [salvation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salvation_\(Christianity\)).[^cite_note-CCC_816-246] Through the [passion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passion_\(Christianity\)) (suffering) of Christ leading to his [crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion_of_Jesus) as described in the Gospels, it is said Christ made himself an oblation to God the Father to [reconcile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atonement_in_Christianity) humanity to God;[^cite_note-608_-247] the [Resurrection of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resurrection_of_Jesus) makes him the firstborn from the dead, the first among many brethren.[^cite_note-248] By reconciling with God and following Christ's words and deeds, an individual can enter the [Kingdom of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_God_\(Christianity\)).[^cite_note-OneFaith26-249] The church sees its liturgy and sacraments as perpetuating the graces achieved through Christ's sacrifice to strengthen a person's relationship with Christ and aid in overcoming sin.[^cite_note-CoCCC_Paschal-250]

### Final judgement

Main article: [Last Judgment § Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Judgment#Catholicism)

The Catholic Church teaches that, immediately after death, the [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul) of each person will receive a [particular judgement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular_judgement) from God, based on their [sins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sin) and their relationship to Christ.[^cite_note-251][^cite_note-Schreck397-252] This teaching also attests to another day when Christ will sit in universal judgement of all mankind. This [final judgement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_judgement), according to the Church's teaching, will bring an end to human history and mark the beginning of both a new and better heaven and earth ruled by God in righteousness.[^cite_note-253]

Depending on the judgement rendered following death, it is believed that a soul may enter one of three states of the afterlife:

- [Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heaven_in_Christianity#Roman_Catholicism) is a state of unending union with the divine nature of God, not ontologically, but by grace. It is an eternal life, in which the soul contemplates God in ceaseless [beatitude](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beatific_vision).

  [^cite_note-254]

- [Purgatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purgatory) is a temporary condition for the purification of souls who, although destined for Heaven, are not fully detached from sin and thus cannot enter Heaven immediately.

  [^cite_note-255]

  &#x20;In Purgatory, the soul suffers, and is purged and perfected. Souls in purgatory may be aided in reaching heaven by the prayers of the faithful on earth and by the [intercession of saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercession_of_saints).

  [^cite_note-256]

- [Final Damnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hell_in_Christian_beliefs#Roman_Catholicism): Finally, those who persist in living in a state of mortal sin and do not repent before death subject themselves to hell, an everlasting separation from God.

  [^cite_note-257]

  &#x20;The church teaches that no one is condemned to hell without having freely decided to reject God.

  [^cite_note-258]

  &#x20;No one is [predestined](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Predestined) to hell and no one can determine with absolute certainty who has been condemned to hell.

  [^cite_note-259]

  &#x20;Catholicism teaches that through God's mercy a person can repent at any point before death, be illuminated with the truth of the Catholic faith, and thus obtain salvation.

  [^cite_note-Luke23-260]

  &#x20;Some Catholic theologians have speculated that the souls of unbaptized infants and non-Christians without mortal sin but who die in [original sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_sin) are assigned to [limbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limbo), although this is not an official [dogma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogma_in_the_Catholic_Church) of the church.

  [^cite_note-261]

While the Catholic Church teaches that it alone possesses the full means of salvation,[^cite_note-CCC_816-246] it also acknowledges that the Holy Spirit can make use of [Christian communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesial_Community) separated from itself to "impel towards Catholic unity"[^cite_note-ewtn.com-262] and "tend and lead toward the Catholic Church",[^cite_note-ewtn.com-262] and thus bring people to salvation, because these separated communities contain some elements of proper doctrine, albeit admixed with [errors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heresy). It teaches that anyone who is saved is saved through the Catholic Church but that people can be saved outside of the ordinary means known as [baptism of desire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_desire), and by pre-baptismal martyrdom, known as [baptism of blood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism_of_blood), as well as when conditions of [invincible ignorance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invincible_ignorance) are present, although invincible ignorance in itself is not a means of salvation.[^cite_note-263] The [Vatican II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vatican_II) document [*Lumen* *gentium*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumen_gentium) further clarifies the possibility of salvation of those who "through no fault of their own, do not know the Gospel of Christ or his Church, but who nevertheless seek God with a sincere heart," being "moved by (divine) grace".["*CCC*, 847"](https://www.vatican.va/archive/ENG0015/__P29.HTM). Vatican.va.

### Saints and devotions

Main articles: [Saint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint), [Canonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonization), [Veneration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration), and [Catholic devotions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_devotions)

A saint (also historically known as a hallow) is a person who is recognized as having an exceptional degree of holiness or likeness or closeness to God, while canonization is the act by which a Christian church declares that a person who has died was a saint, upon which declaration the person is included in the "canon", or list, of recognized saints.[^cite_note-WilsonFischer2005-264][^cite_note-DiehlDonnelly2001-265] The first persons honoured as saints were the [martyrs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_martyr). By the fourth century, however, "[confessors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confessor_of_the_Faith)"—people who had confessed their faith not by dying but by suffering—began to be venerated publicly.[^cite_note-ce_beccari-266]

In the Catholic Church, both in Latin and Eastern Catholic churches, the act of canonization is reserved to the [Apostolic See](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_See) and occurs at the conclusion of a long process requiring extensive proof that the candidate for canonization lived and died in such an exemplary and holy way that he is worthy to be recognized as a saint. The church's official recognition of sanctity implies that the person is now in [Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heaven) and that he may be publicly invoked and mentioned officially in the [liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgy) of the church, including in the [Litany of the Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Litany_of_the_Saints). [Canonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonization) allows universal veneration of the saint in the liturgy of the [Roman Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Rite); for permission to venerate at a local level only [beatification](233724.md) is needed.[^cite_note-267]

[Devotions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_devotions) are "external practices of piety" which are not part of the official liturgy of the Catholic Church but are part of the popular spiritual practices of Catholics.[^cite_note-268] These include various practices regarding the veneration of the saints, especially [veneration of the Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church). Other devotional practices include the [Stations of the Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stations_of_the_Cross), the [Sacred Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacred_Heart) of Jesus, the [Holy Face of Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Face_of_Jesus),[^cite_note-etwndevoti-269] the various [scapulars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scapular), novenas to various saints,[^cite_note-popdevos-270] [pilgrimages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage)[^cite_note-271] and devotions to the [Blessed Sacrament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist_\(Catholic_Church\)),[^cite_note-popdevos-270] and the veneration of saintly images such as the *[santos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santo_\(art\))*.[^cite_note-272] The bishops at the Second Vatican Council reminded Catholics that "devotions should be so drawn up that they harmonize with the liturgical seasons, accord with the sacred liturgy, are in some fashion derived from it, and lead the people to it, since, in fact, the liturgy by its very nature far surpasses any of them."[^cite_note-273]

Main articles: [Veneration of Mary in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church), [Mariology of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariology_of_the_Catholic_Church), [Mariology of the saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariology_of_the_saints), and [Mariology of the popes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariology_of_the_popes)



The [Blessed Virgin Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneration_of_Mary_in_the_Catholic_Church) is highly regarded in the Catholic Church, proclaiming her as [Mother of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_God), [free from original sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immaculate_Conception) and an [intercessor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercessor).

[Catholic Mariology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Mariology) deals with the [dogmas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dogma_in_the_Catholic_Church) and teachings concerning the life of [Mary, mother of Jesus](73513.md), as well as the veneration of Mary by the faithful. Mary is held in special regard, declared the [Mother of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_God) ([Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language): Θεοτόκος, [romanized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanization_of_Greek): *Theotokos*, [lit.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literal_translation) 'God-bearer'), and believed as dogma to have remained a [virgin throughout her life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perpetual_virginity_of_Mary).[^cite_note-ReferenceB-274] Further teachings include the doctrines of the [Immaculate Conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immaculate_Conception) (her own conception without the stain of original sin) and the [Assumption of Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assumption_of_Mary) (that her body was assumed directly into heaven at the end of her life). Both of these doctrines were defined as infallible dogma, by [Pope Pius IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX) in 1854 and [Pope Pius XII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII) in 1950 respectively,[^cite_note-Barry,_p._106-275] but only after consulting with the Catholic bishops throughout the world to ascertain that this is a Catholic belief.[^cite_note-276] In the Eastern Catholic churches, however, they continue to celebrate the feast of the Assumption under the name of the [Dormition of the Mother of God](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dormition_of_the_Mother_of_God) on the same date.[^cite_note-277] The teaching that Mary died before being assumed significantly precedes the idea that she did not. St John Damascene wrote that "St Juvenal, Bishop of Jerusalem, at the Council of Chalcedon (451), made known to the Emperor Marcian and Pulcheria, who wished to possess the body of the Mother of God, that Mary died in the presence of all the Apostles, but that her tomb, when opened, upon the request of St Thomas, was found empty; wherefrom the Apostles concluded that the body was taken up to Heaven."[^cite_note-278]

[Devotions to Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_devotions) are part of Catholic piety but are distinct from the worship of God.[^cite_note-279] Practices include prayers and [Marian art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_art), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Marian_music) and [architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Marian_church_buildings). Several [liturgical Marian feasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_feast_days) are celebrated throughout the [Church Year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_Year) and she is honoured with [many titles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titles_of_Mary) such as [Queen of Heaven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_of_Heaven). [Pope Paul VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI) called her [Mother of the Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_of_the_Church) because, by giving birth to Christ, she is considered to be the spiritual mother to each member of the [Body of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Body_of_Christ).[^cite_note-Barry,_p._106-275] Because of her influential role in the life of Jesus, prayers and devotions such as the [Hail Mary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hail_Mary), the [Rosary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosary), the [Salve Regina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salve_Regina) and the [Memorare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorare) are common Catholic practices.[^cite_note-280] [Pilgrimage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_pilgrimage) to the sites of several [Marian apparitions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marian_apparitions) affirmed by the church, such as [Lourdes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Lourdes), [Fátima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_F%C3%A1tima) and [Guadalupe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Our_Lady_of_Guadalupe),[^cite_note-281] are also popular Catholic devotions.[^cite_note-282]

## Sacraments

Main article: [Sacraments of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacraments_of_the_Catholic_Church)



[Holy Mass](5684685.md) at the [Sanctuary of Our Lady of Fátima](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sanctuary_of_Our_Lady_of_F%C3%A1tima), Portugal. The [host](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramental_bread) and the [chalice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalice) are displayed to the people immediately after the [consecration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecration_in_Christianity) of the bread and wine into the [Holy Body and Blood of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist).

The Catholic Church teaches that it was entrusted with seven sacraments that were instituted by Christ. The number and nature of the sacraments were defined by several [ecumenical councils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecumenical_council), most recently the Council of Trent.[^cite_note-1113_14-283][^cite_note-284] These are [baptism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baptism), [confirmation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation), the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist), [penance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrament_of_Penance_\(Catholic_Church\)), [anointing of the sick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anointing_of_the_Sick_\(Catholic_Church\)) (formerly called Extreme Unction, one of the "[Last Rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Rites)"), [holy orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_orders) and [holy matrimony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_marriage). Sacraments are visible rituals that Catholics see as signs of God's presence and effective channels of God's [grace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_\(Christianity\)) to all those who receive them with the proper disposition (*[ex opere operato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ex_opere_operato)*).[^cite_note-285] The *[Catechism of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church)* categorizes the sacraments into three groups, the "sacraments of Christian initiation", "sacraments of healing" and "sacraments at the service of communion and the mission of the faithful". These groups broadly reflect the stages of people's natural and spiritual lives which each sacrament is intended to serve.[^cite_note-286]

The liturgies of the sacraments are central to the church's mission. According to the *Catechism*:

> &#x20;In the liturgy of the New Covenant every liturgical action, especially the celebration of the Eucharist and the sacraments, is an encounter between Christ and the Church. The liturgical assembly derives its unity from the "communion of the Holy Spirit" who gathers the children of God into the one Body of Christ. This assembly transcends racial, cultural, social—indeed, all human affinities.[^cite_note-287]

According to church doctrine, the sacraments of the church require the proper form, matter, and intent to be validly celebrated.[^cite_note-288] In addition, the [Canon Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_Law) for both the Latin Church and the Eastern Catholic Churches govern who may licitly celebrate certain sacraments, as well as strict rules about who may receive the sacraments.[^cite_note-CoCC291-289] Notably, because the church teaches that Christ is [present](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_presence#Catholic_and_Orthodox) in the Eucharist,[^cite_note-Kreeft326-290] those who are conscious of being in a state of mortal sin are forbidden to receive the sacrament until they have received absolution through the [sacrament of Reconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrament_of_Reconciliation) (Penance).[^cite_note-Kreeft331-291] Catholics are normally obliged to abstain from eating for at least an hour before receiving the sacrament.[^cite_note-Kreeft331-291] Non-Catholics are ordinarily prohibited from receiving the Eucharist as well.[^cite_note-CoCC291-289][^cite_note-292]

Catholics, even if they were in danger of death and unable to approach a Catholic minister, may not ask for the sacraments of the Eucharist, penance or anointing of the sick from someone, such as a Protestant minister, who is not known to be validly ordained in line with Catholic teaching on ordination.[^cite_note-293][^cite_note-294] Likewise, even in grave and pressing need, Catholic ministers may not administer these sacraments to those who do not manifest Catholic faith in the sacrament. In relation to the churches of Eastern Christianity not in communion with the Holy See, the Catholic Church is less restrictive, declaring that "a certain *communion in sacris*, and so in the Eucharist, given suitable circumstances and the approval of Church authority, is not merely possible but is encouraged."[^cite_note-295]

### Sacraments of initiation

Main article: [Sacraments of initiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacraments_of_initiation)

#### Baptism



Baptism of [Augustine of Hippo](2030.md) as represented in a sculptural group in [Troyes Cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troyes_Cathedral) (1549), France

As viewed by the Catholic Church, baptism is the first of three sacraments of initiation as a Christian.[^cite_note-296] It washes away all sins, both [original sin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Original_sin) and personal actual sins.[^cite_note-297] It makes a person a member of the church.[^cite_note-298] As a gratuitous gift of God that requires no merit on the part of the person who is baptized, it is [conferred even on children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infant_baptism),[^cite_note-299] who, though they have no personal sins, need it on account of original sin.[^cite_note-300]

If a new-born child is in a danger of death, anyone—be it a doctor, a nurse, or a parent—may baptize the child.[^cite_note-301] Baptism marks a person permanently and cannot be repeated.[^cite_note-302] The Catholic Church recognizes as valid baptisms conferred even by people who are not Catholics or Christians, provided that they intend to baptize ("to do what the Church does when she baptizes") and that they use the [Trinitarian baptismal formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinitarian_formula#Use_in_baptism).[^cite_note-303]

#### Confirmation

Main article: [Confirmation in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_in_the_Catholic_Church)

The Catholic Church sees the sacrament of confirmation as required to complete the grace given in baptism.[^cite_note-304] When adults are baptized, confirmation is normally given immediately afterwards,[^cite_note-cann883-305] a practice followed even with newly baptized infants in the Eastern Catholic Churches.[^cite_note-cceo695-306] In the West confirmation of children is delayed until they are old enough to understand or at the bishop's discretion.[^cite_note-cann891-307] In Western Christianity, particularly Catholicism, the sacrament is called *confirmation*, because it confirms and strengthens the grace of baptism; in the Eastern Churches, it is called *chrismation*, because the essential rite is the anointing of the person with [chrism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chrism),[^cite_note-Chrism-308] a mixture of [olive oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olive_oil) and some perfumed substance, usually [balsam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oleoresin), blessed by a bishop.[^cite_note-Chrism-308][^cite_note-309] Those who receive confirmation must be in a state of grace, which for those who have reached the [age of reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_reason_\(canon_law\)) means that they should first be cleansed spiritually by the sacrament of Penance; they should also have the intention of receiving the sacrament, and be prepared to show in their lives that they are Christians.[^cite_note-310]

#### Eucharist

Main article: [Eucharist in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist_in_the_Catholic_Church)



[Pope Benedict XVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XVI) celebrates the Eucharist at the [canonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canonization) of [Frei Galvão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frei_Galv%C3%A3o) in São Paulo, Brazil on 11 May 2007.

For Catholics, the Eucharist is the sacrament which completes Christian initiation. It is described as "the source and summit of the Christian life".[^cite_note-311] The ceremony in which a Catholic first receives the Eucharist is known as [First Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Communion).[^cite_note-312]

The Eucharistic celebration, also called the [Mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_\(liturgy\)) or [Divine liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_liturgy), includes prayers and scriptural readings, as well as an offering of bread and wine, which are brought to the [altar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Altar) and [consecrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consecration#Eucharist) by the priest to become the body and the blood of Jesus Christ: the [substance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substance_\(philosophy\)) of bread and wine changes despite the [accidents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accident_\(philosophy\)) (e.g., the visible form) remaining: a change called [transubstantiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transubstantiation).[^cite_note-313]

The [words of consecration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Words_of_consecration) reflect the words spoken by Jesus during the [Last Supper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_Supper), where Christ offered his body and blood to his Apostles the night before his crucifixion. The sacrament re-presents (makes present) the sacrifice of Jesus on the cross,[^cite_note-314] and perpetuates it. Christ's death and resurrection give grace through the sacrament that unites the faithful with Christ and one another, remits venial sin, and aids against committing moral sin (though mortal sin itself is forgiven through the sacrament of penance).[^cite_note-ccc1392-315]



A Catholic prays in a church in Mexico.

### Sacraments of healing

#### Penance

Main article: [Sacrament of Penance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacrament_of_Penance)

The sacrament of penance (also called Reconciliation, Forgiveness, Confession, and Conversion[^cite_note-316]) exists for the conversion of those who, after baptism, separate themselves from Christ by sin.[^cite_note-317] Essential to this sacrament are acts both by the sinner (examination of conscience, contrition with a determination not to sin again, confession to a priest, and performance of some act to repair the damage caused by sin) and by the priest (determination of the act of reparation to be performed and [absolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolution)).[^cite_note-318]

Serious sins ([mortal sins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortal_sin)) should be confessed at least once a year and always before receiving the Eucharist, while confession of [venial sins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venial_sin) also is recommended.[^cite_note-319] The priest is bound under the severest penalties to maintain the "[seal of confession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_of_confession)", absolute secrecy about any sins revealed to him in confession.[^cite_note-320]

#### Anointing of the sick

Main article: [Anointing of the Sick in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anointing_of_the_Sick_in_the_Catholic_Church)



The *[Seven Sacraments Altarpiece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_Sacraments_Altarpiece)* [triptych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triptych) painting of Extreme Unction (anointing of the sick) with oil being administered by a priest during last rites. [Rogier van der Weyden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogier_van_der_Weyden), 1445.

While chrism is used only for the three sacraments that cannot be repeated, a different oil is used by a priest or bishop to bless a Catholic who, because of illness or old age, has begun to be in danger of death.[^cite_note-321] This sacrament, known as anointing of the sick, is believed to give comfort, peace, courage and, if the sick person is unable to make a confession, even forgiveness of sins.[^cite_note-322]

The sacrament is also referred to as *unction*, and in the past as *extreme unction*, and it is one of the three sacraments that constitute the [last rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Last_rites), together with Penance and [Viaticum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viaticum) (Eucharist).[^cite_note-323]

### Sacraments at the service of communion

According to the Catechism, there are two sacraments of [communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koinonia) directed towards the salvation of others: priesthood and marriage.[^cite_note-324] Within the general vocation to be a Christian, these two sacraments "consecrate to specific mission or [vocation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vocation) among the people of God. Men receive the holy orders to feed the church by the word and [grace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grace_\(Christianity\)). Spouses marry so that their love may be fortified to fulfil duties of their state".[^cite_note-325]

#### Holy orders

Main article: [Holy orders in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_orders_in_the_Catholic_Church)



Priests lay their hands on the ordinands during the rite of ordination.

The sacrament of holy orders consecrates and deputes some Christians to serve the whole body as members of three degrees or orders: episcopate (bishops), presbyterate (priests) and diaconate (deacons).[^cite_note-cann10081009-326][^cite_note-327] The church has defined rules on who may be ordained into the [clergy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clergy#Catholicism). In the Latin Church the priesthood is generally restricted to celibate men, and the episcopate is always restricted to celibate men.[^cite_note-328] Men who are already married may be ordained in certain Eastern Catholic churches in most countries,[^cite_note-329] and the personal ordinariates and may become deacons even in the Latin Church[^cite_note-CCL1031-330][^cite_note-CCL1037-331] (see [clerical marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clerical_marriage)). After becoming a Catholic priest, a man may not marry (see [clerical celibacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clerical_celibacy)) unless he is formally [laicized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laicized).[^cite_note-332]

All clergy, whether deacons, priests or bishops, may preach, teach, baptize, witness marriages and conduct funeral liturgies.[^cite_note-333] Only bishops and priests can administer the sacraments of the Eucharist, reconciliation (penance) and anointing of the sick.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] Only bishops can administer the sacrament of Holy Orders, which [ordains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordination) someone into the clergy.[^cite_note-OneFaith114-336]

#### Matrimony

Main article: [Marriage in the Catholic Church](395673.md)

See also: [Catholic teachings on sexual morality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_teachings_on_sexual_morality)



Wedding mass in the [Philippines](23440.md)

The Catholic Church teaches that marriage is a social and spiritual bond between a man and a woman, ordered towards the good of the spouses and procreation of children; according to Catholic teachings on sexual morality, it is the only appropriate context for sexual activity. A Catholic marriage, or any marriage between baptized individuals of any Christian denomination, is viewed as a sacrament. A sacramental marriage, once consummated, cannot be dissolved except by death.[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-340] The church recognizes certain [conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_\(Catholic_Church\)#Conditions_for_a_valid_marriage_of_Catholics), such as freedom of consent, as required for any marriage to be valid; In addition, the church sets specific rules and norms, known as [canonical form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marriage_\(Catholic_Church\)#Canonical_form), that Catholics must follow.[^cite_note-341]

The church does not recognize divorce as ending a valid marriage and allows state-recognized divorce only as a means of protecting the property and well-being of the spouses and any children. However, consideration of particular cases by the competent ecclesiastical tribunal can lead to declaration of the invalidity of a marriage, a declaration usually referred to as an [annulment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annulment_\(Catholic_Church\)). Remarriage following a divorce is not permitted unless the prior marriage was declared invalid.[^cite_note-342]

## Liturgy

Main articles: [Catholic liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_liturgy) and [Eastern Catholic liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_liturgy)



Catholic religious objects – [Holy Bible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Bible), [crucifix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifix) and [rosary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosary)

Among the 24 autonomous (*[sui iuris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sui_iuris)*) churches, numerous liturgical and other traditions exist, called rites, which reflect historical and cultural diversity rather than differences in belief.[^cite_note-343] In the definition of the [Code of Canons of the Eastern Churches](3547385.md), "a rite is the liturgical, theological, spiritual, and disciplinary patrimony, culture and circumstances of history of a distinct people, by which its own manner of living the faith is manifested in each Church *sui iuris*".[^cite_note-CCEO28-344]

The liturgy of the sacrament of the [Eucharist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eucharist_in_the_Catholic_Church), called the [Mass](5684685.md) in the West and [Divine Liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Liturgy) or other names in the East, is the principal liturgy of the Catholic Church.[^cite_note-345] This is because it is considered the propitiatory sacrifice of Christ himself.[^cite_note-346] Eastern Catholic Churches have their [own rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_liturgy): the liturgies of the Eucharist and the other sacraments vary from rite to rite, reflecting different theological emphases.

### Western rites

Main articles: [Roman Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Rite) and [Latin liturgical rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_liturgical_rites)

| Part of *[a series](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:Mass_in_the_Catholic_Church)* on |

The [Roman Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Rite) is the most common [rite of worship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_liturgical_rites) used by the Catholic Church, with the [Ordinary Form of the Roman Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinary_Form_of_the_Roman_Rite) form of the Mass. Its use is found worldwide, originating in Rome and spreading throughout Europe, influencing and eventually supplanting local rites.[^cite_note-347] The present ordinary form of Mass in the Roman Rite, found in the post-1969 editions of the [Roman Missal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Missal), is usually celebrated in the local [vernacular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vernacular) language, using an officially approved translation from the original text in [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin).[^cite_note-348]

Its most widely used [form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_of_Paul_VI) was [promulgated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Missale_Romanum_\(apostolic_constitution\)) by [Pope Paul VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI) in 1969 and [revised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgiam_Authenticam) by [Pope John Paul II](23805.md) in 2001. In certain circumstances, the [1962 form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1962_Roman_Missal) of the Roman Rite remains authorized in the Latin Church.

Since 2014, clergy in the small [personal ordinariates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_ordinariate) set up for groups of former Anglicans under the terms of the 2009 document *[Anglicanorum Coetibus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanorum_Coetibus)*[^cite_note-Anglicanorum_Coetibus-349] are permitted to use a variation of the Roman Rite called "Divine Worship" or, less formally, "Ordinariate Use",[^cite_note-350] which incorporates elements of the [Anglican liturgy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_liturgy#Anglican_Communion) and traditions.[^cite_note-351]

In the [Archdiocese of Milan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Milan), with around five million Catholics the largest in Europe,[^cite_note-352] Mass is celebrated according to the [Ambrosian Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambrosian_Rite). Other [Latin Church rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_liturgical_rites) include the [Mozarabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mozarabic_Rite)[^cite_note-353] and those of some religious institutes.[^cite_note-354] These liturgical rites have an antiquity of at least 200 years before 1570, the date of Pope Pius V's *[Quo primum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quo_primum)*, and were thus allowed to continue.[^cite_note-355]

### Eastern rites

Main article: [Catholic particular churches and liturgical rites § Eastern rites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_particular_churches_and_liturgical_rites#Eastern_rites)



[East Syrian Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Syrian_Rite) [wedding crowning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wedding_crowning) celebrated by a bishop of the [Syro-Malabar Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syro-Malabar_Catholic_Church) in India, one of the 23 Eastern Catholic Churches in [full communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_communion) with the pope and the Catholic Church

The Eastern Catholic Churches share common patrimony and liturgical rites as their counterparts, including [Eastern Orthodox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox) and other [Eastern Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Christian) churches who are no longer in communion with the Holy See. These include churches that historically developed in Russia, Caucasus, the Balkans, North Eastern Africa, India and the Middle East. The Eastern Catholic Churches are groups of faithful who have either never been out of communion with the Holy See or who have restored communion with it at the cost of breaking communion with their associates of the same tradition.[^cite_note-356]

The [liturgical rites of the Eastern Catholic Churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_liturgy) include the [Byzantine Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Rite) (in its Antiochian, Greek and Slavonic [recensions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Use_\(liturgy\))), the [Alexandrian Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandrian_Rite), the [West Syrian Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Syrian_Rite), the [Armenian Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armenian_Rite), and the [East Syriac Rite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Syriac_Rite). Eastern Catholic Churches have the autonomy to set the particulars of their liturgical forms and worship, within certain limits to protect the "accurate observance" of their liturgical tradition.[^cite_note-357]

In the past, some of the rites used by the Eastern Catholic Churches were subject to a degree of [liturgical Latinization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_Latinization). In recent years Eastern Catholic Churches have returned to traditional Eastern practices in accord with the 1964 [Vatican II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vatican_II) decree *[Orientalium Ecclesiarum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orientalium_Ecclesiarum)*.[^cite_note-358] Each church has its own [liturgical calendar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liturgical_calendar).[^cite_note-359]

## Social, moral and cultural issues

### Catholic social teaching

Main article: [Catholic social teaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_social_teaching)



On 24 May 2015, [Pope Francis](1687680.md) issued the *[Laudato si'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laudato_si')*, an [encyclical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Encyclical) that deals with questions such as consumerism, responsible development and environmental degradation.

Catholic social teaching, reflecting the concern Jesus showed for the impoverished, places a heavy emphasis on the [corporal works of mercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_works_of_mercy) and the [spiritual works of mercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiritual_works_of_mercy), namely the support and concern for the sick, the poor and the afflicted.[^cite_note-360][^cite_note-Compendium_of_the_CCC,_388-361] Church teaching calls for a preferential [option for the poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Option_for_the_poor) while canon law prescribes that "The Christian faithful are also obliged to promote [social justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_justice) and, mindful of the precept of the Lord, to assist the poor."[^cite_note-Cann22-362] Its foundations are widely considered to have been laid by Pope Leo XIII's 1891 encyclical letter *[Rerum novarum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rerum_novarum)* which upholds the rights and dignity of labour and the right of workers to form unions.[^cite_note-363][^cite_note-364]

### Social services

Main articles: [Catholic Church and health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_health_care) and [Catholic education](1452366.md)



&#x20;[Saint Teresa of Calcutta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_Teresa) advocated for the sick, the poor and the needy by practising the acts of [corporal works of mercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corporal_works_of_mercy). She was awarded the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md) in 1979.

The Catholic Church is the largest non-government provider of education and medical services in the world.[^cite_note-Geopolitics-216] In 2010 the Catholic Church's Pontifical Council for Pastoral Assistance to Health Care Workers said that the church manages 26% of health care facilities in the world, including hospitals, clinics, orphanages, pharmacies and centres for those with leprosy.[^cite_note-365]

The church has always been involved in education, since the founding of the [first universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_university) of Europe.[^cite_note-auto-76] It runs and sponsors thousands of primary and secondary schools, [colleges and universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_higher_education) throughout the world[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-Vermont_winter-7] and operates the world's largest non-governmental school system.[^cite_note-367]

Religious institutes for women have played a particularly prominent role in the provision of health and education services,[^cite_note-nunsworldwide-368] as with orders such as the [Sisters of Mercy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisters_of_Mercy), [Little Sisters of the Poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Sisters_of_the_Poor), the Missionaries of Charity, the [Sisters of St. Joseph of the Sacred Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisters_of_St._Joseph_of_the_Sacred_Heart), the [Sisters of the Blessed Sacrament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sisters_of_the_Blessed_Sacrament) and the [Daughters of Charity of Saint Vincent de Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daughters_of_Charity_of_Saint_Vincent_de_Paul).[^cite_note-369] The Catholic nun [Mother Teresa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_Teresa) of [Calcutta, India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcutta,_India), founder of the Missionaries of Charity, was awarded the [Nobel Peace Prize](26230922.md) in 1979 for her humanitarian work among India's poor.[^cite_note-370] Bishop [Carlos Filipe Ximenes Belo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Filipe_Ximenes_Belo) won the same award in 1996 for "work towards a just and peaceful solution to the conflict in [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor)".[^cite_note-371]

The church is also actively engaged in international aid and development through organizations such as [Catholic Relief Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Relief_Services), [Caritas Internationalis](47756384.md), [Aid to the Church in Need](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aid_to_the_Church_in_Need), refugee advocacy groups such as the [Jesuit Refugee Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesuit_Refugee_Service) and community aid groups such as the [Saint Vincent de Paul Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Vincent_de_Paul_Society).[^cite_note-372]

### Sexual morality

Main articles: [Catholic theology of sexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_theology_of_sexuality), [Catholic theology of the body](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_theology_of_the_body), and [Marriage in the Catholic Church](395673.md)



An [allegory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegory) of chastity by [Hans Memling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Memling)

#### Chastity and marriage

In the church's teaching, sexual activity should be reserved to married couples without artificial birth control. Marriage is considered the only appropriate context for sexual activity,[^cite_note-2337_-373] whether in a [sacramental marriage](395673.md) among Christians or in a [natural marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_marriage) where one or both spouses are unbaptized. Even in romantic relationships, including [engagement to marriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engagement_to_marriage), partners are called to abstain from sexual activity, in order to test mutual respect and fidelity.[^cite_note-374]

Chastity in marriage requires, in particular, conjugal fidelity and protecting the fecundity of marriage. The couple must foster trust and honesty as well as spiritual and physical intimacy. Sexual activity must always be open to the transmission of new life;[^cite_note-375] the church calls this the procreative significance. It must likewise always bring a couple together in love; the church calls this the unitive significance.[^cite_note-humanaevitae-376] Artificial [contraception](18978770.md) and certain other [sexual practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_teachings_on_sexual_morality#Teachings_on_specific_subjects) are not permitted, although [natural family planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_family_planning) methods are permitted to provide healthy spacing between births, or to postpone children for a just reason.[^cite_note-377]

Church teachings about sexuality have become an issue of increasing controversy in the Western world, especially after the close of the Second Vatican Council in the 1960s, due to changing cultural attitudes described as the [sexual revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_revolution).[^cite_note-humanaevitae-376] Pope Francis said in 2015 that he was worried that the church has grown "obsessed" with issues such as abortion, [same-sex marriage](92656.md) and [contraception](18978770.md), and for prioritizing moral doctrines over helping the poor and marginalized.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379]

#### Homosexuality

Main article: [Homosexuality and the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homosexuality_and_the_Catholic_Church)

The Catholic Church teaches that "homosexual acts" are "contrary to the natural law", "acts of grave depravity" and "under no circumstances can they be approved", but that persons experiencing homosexual tendencies must be accorded respect and dignity.[^cite_note-ccc2357-2359-380] According to the *[Catechism of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church)*,

> The number of men and women who have deep-seated homosexual tendencies is not negligible. This inclination, which is objectively disordered, constitutes for most of them a trial. They must be accepted with respect, compassion, and sensitivity. Every sign of unjust discrimination in their regard should be avoided... Homosexual persons are called to chastity. By the virtues of self-mastery that teach them inner freedom, at times by the support of disinterested friendship, by prayer and sacramental grace, they can and should gradually and resolutely approach Christian perfection.[^cite_note-ccc2357-2359-380][^cite_note-385]

Orthodox Catholic groups, such as Building Catholic Futures, encourage parishes to incorporate celibate gay people into the church communities.[^cite_note-386] Certain [dissenting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissent) Catholic groups, such as [DignityUSA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DignityUSA), [oppose the position of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dissent_from_Catholic_teaching_on_homosexuality) and seek to change it and to educate Catholics on LGBT issues.[^cite_note-387] The Catholic Church has banned all such groups from church property.[^cite_note-388]

#### Divorce and declarations of nullity

Main article: [Declaration of nullity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_of_nullity)

Further information: [Divorce law by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divorce_law_by_country)

Canon law makes no provision for divorce between baptized individuals, as a valid, consummated sacramental marriage is considered to be a lifelong bond.[^cite_note-389] However, a declaration of nullity may be granted when the proof is produced that essential conditions for contracting a valid marriage were absent from the beginning—in other words, that the marriage was not valid due to some impediment. A declaration of nullity, commonly called an annulment, is a judgement on the part of an [ecclesiastical tribunal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecclesiastical_tribunal) determining that a marriage was invalidly attempted.[^cite_note-390]

Marriages among unbaptized individuals may be dissolved with papal permission under certain situations, such as a desire to marry a Catholic, under [Pauline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_privilege) or [Petrine privilege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrine_privilege).[^cite_note-Gantley-338][^cite_note-Doors-339] An attempt at remarriage following divorce without a declaration of nullity places "the remarried spouse ... in a situation of public and permanent adultery". An innocent spouse who lives in continence following divorce, or couples who live in continence following a civil divorce for a grave cause, do not sin.[^cite_note-391]

Worldwide, diocesan tribunals completed over 49000 cases for nullity of marriage in 2006. Over the past 30 years about 55 to 70% of annulments have occurred in the United States. The growth in annulments has been substantial; in the United States, 27,000 marriages were annulled in 2006, compared to 338 in 1968. However, approximately 200,000 married Catholics in the United States divorce each year; 10 million total as of 2006.[^cite_note-Soule-392][^cite_note-394] Divorce is increasing in some predominantly Catholic countries in Europe.[^cite_note-395]

#### Contraception and abortion

Main article: [Christian views on birth control § Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_views_on_birth_control#Catholicism)

See also: [Catholic Church and HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_HIV/AIDS) and [Religious response to assisted reproductive technology § Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_response_to_assisted_reproductive_technology#Catholicism)



[Pope Paul VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI) issued *[Humanae vitae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanae_vitae)* on 25 July 1968.

The church teaches that [sexual intercourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_reproduction#Copulation) should only take place between a man and woman who are married to each other, and should be without the use of [birth control](18978770.md) or [contraception](18978770.md). In his encyclical *[Humanae vitae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humanae_vitae)*[^cite_note-humanae-396] (1968) [Pope Paul VI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Paul_VI) firmly rejected all artificial contraception, thus contradicting dissenters in the church who saw the [birth control pill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Birth_control_pill) as an ethically justifiable method of contraception, though he permitted the regulation of births by means of natural family planning (NFP.) This teaching was continued especially by John Paul II in his encyclical *_[Evangelium Vitae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evangelium_Vitae)_*, where he clarified the church's position on artificial contraception, [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_abortion) and [euthanasia](9587.md) by condemning them as part of a "culture of death" and calling instead for a "[culture of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_life)".[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBokenkotter200427,_154,_493–94-397]

Many Western Catholics have voiced significant disagreement with the church's teaching on contraception.[^cite_note-398] Overturning the church's teaching on this point features high on progressive agendas.[^cite_note-399] [Catholics for Choice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholics_for_Choice), a political lobbyist group that is not associated with the Catholic Church, stated in 1998 that 96% of American Catholic women had used contraceptives at some point in their lives and that 72% of Catholics believed that one could be a good Catholic without obeying the church's teaching on birth control.[^cite_note-cath_choice-400] Use of natural family planning methods among United States Catholics purportedly is low, although the number cannot be known with certainty.[^cite_note-403] As Catholic health providers are among the largest providers of services to patients with [HIV/AIDS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HIV/AIDS) worldwide, there is significant controversy within and outside the church regarding the use of condoms as a means of limiting new infections, as [condom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Condom) use ordinarily constitutes prohibited contraceptive use.[^cite_note-CNS.AIDS-404]

Similarly, the Catholic Church opposes [artificial insemination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_insemination) regardless of whether it is homologous (from the husband) or heterologous (from a [donor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sperm_donation)) and [in vitro fertilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/In_vitro_fertilization) (IVF), saying that the artificial process replaces the love and conjugal act between a husband and wife.[^cite_note-405] In addition, it opposes IVF because it might cause disposal of embryos; Catholics believe an embryo is an individual with a [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul) who must be treated as such.[^cite_note-406] For this reason, the church also opposes abortion.[^cite_note-407]

The Catholic Church oppose all forms of [abortion](765.md) procedures whose direct purpose is to destroy a [zygote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zygote), [blastocyst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blastocyst), [embryo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryo) or [fetus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fetus), since it holds that "human life must be respected and protected absolutely from the moment of [conception](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertilisation). From the first moment of his existence, a human being must be recognized as having the rights of a person – among which is the inviolable [right of every innocent being to life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_life)".[^cite_note-Catechism-408] However, the church does recognize as morally legitimate certain acts which [indirectly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indirect_abortion) result in the death of the fetus. The [1983 *Code of Canon Law*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1983_Code_of_Canon_Law) imposes [automatic (*latae sententiae*)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latae_sententiae) [excommunication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excommunication_\(Catholic_Church\)) on [Latin Catholics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin_Catholics) who actually procure an abortion,[^cite_note-409] if they fulfill the conditions for being subject to such a sanction.[^cite_note-410]

&#x20;Due to the anti-abortion stance, some Catholics oppose receiving vaccines derived from fetal cells obtained via abortion. On 21 December 2020, and regarding [COVID-19 vaccination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination), the [Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith) emitted a document stating that "it is morally acceptable to receive Covid-19 vaccines that have used cell lines from aborted fetuses in their research and production process" when no alternative vaccine is available, since "the moral duty to avoid such passive material cooperation is not obligatory if there is a grave danger, such as the otherwise uncontainable spread of a serious pathological agent."[^cite_note-411][^cite_note-\:0-412] The document states that receiving the vaccine does not constitute endorsement of the practice of abortion, and that "the morality of vaccination depends not only on the duty to protect one's own health, but also on the duty to pursue the common good."[^cite_note-\:0-412] The document says further:

> &#x20;Those who, however, for reasons of conscience, refuse vaccines produced with cell lines from aborted fetuses, must do their utmost to avoid, by other prophylactic means and appropriate behavior, becoming vehicles for the transmission of the infectious agent. In particular, they must avoid any risk to the health of those who cannot be vaccinated for medical or other reasons, and who are the most vulnerable.[^cite_note-\:0-412]

### Death penalty and euthanasia

Main article: [Catholic Church and capital punishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_capital_punishment)

The Catholic Church is committed to the worldwide abolition of the death penalty in any circumstance.[^cite_note-\:16-413] The current *[Catechism of the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catechism_of_the_Catholic_Church)* teaches that "in the light of the [Gospel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gospel)" the death penalty is "inadmissible because it is an attack on the inviolability and [dignity of the person](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_dignity)" and that the Catholic Church "works with determination for its abolition worldwide."[^cite_note-\:02-414] In his 2020 encyclical *[Fratelli tutti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fratelli_tutti)*, Francis repeated that the death penalty is "inadmissible" and that "there can be no stepping back from this position".[^cite_note-\:112-415] On 9 January 2022 Pope Francis stated in his annual speech to Vatican ambassadors: "The death penalty cannot be employed for a purported state justice, since it does not constitute a deterrent nor render justice to victims, but only fuels the thirst for vengeance".[^cite_note-416]

There is controversy about whether the Catholic Church considers the death penalty intrinsically evil.[^cite_note-\:1-417] The American Archbishop [José Horacio Gómez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Horacio_G%C3%B3mez)[^cite_note-\:1-417] and the Catholic philosopher [Edward Feser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_Feser) argue that this is a matter of prudential judgement and that the church does not teach this as a *de fide* statement;[^cite_note-418] others, such as Cardinals [Charles Maung Bo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Maung_Bo) and [Rino Fisichella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rino_Fisichella), state that it does.[^cite_note-\:1-417]

The Catholic Church opposes active euthanasia and [physician-assisted suicide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physician-assisted_suicide) on the grounds that life is a gift from God and should not be prematurely shortened. However, the church allows dying people to refuse [extraordinary treatments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dysthanasia) that would minimally prolong life without hope of recovery.[^cite_note-Dowbiggin98-419][^cite_note-420]

### Holy orders and women

Main articles: [Ordination of women in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordination_of_women_in_the_Catholic_Church) and [Women in the Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women_in_the_Catholic_Church)

Women and men religious engage in a variety of occupations such as contemplative prayer, teaching, providing health care, and working as missionaries.[^cite_note-nunsworldwide-368][^cite_note-news.bbc.co.uk-421] [Catholic women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_and_women) have played diverse roles in the life of the church, with religious institutes providing a formal space for their participation and [convents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convent) providing spaces for their self-government, prayer and influence through many centuries. [Religious sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religious_sisters) and [nuns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nun) have been extensively involved in developing and running the church's worldwide health and education service networks.[^cite_note-422]

[Holy Orders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Orders) are reserved for men. Efforts in support of the [ordination of women](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_doctrine_on_the_ordination_of_women) to the priesthood led to several rulings by the Roman Curia or popes against the proposal, as in *[Declaration on the Question of the Admission of Women to the Ministerial Priesthood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Declaration_on_the_Question_of_the_Admission_of_Women_to_the_Ministerial_Priesthood)* (1976), *[Mulieris Dignitatem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mulieris_Dignitatem)* (1988) and *[Ordinatio sacerdotalis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ordinatio_sacerdotalis)* (1994). According to the latest ruling, found in *Ordinatio sacerdotalis*, [Pope John Paul II](23805.md) affirmed that the Catholic Church "does not consider herself authorised to admit women to priestly ordination".[^cite_note-423]

In defiance of these rulings, independent opposition groups such as [Roman Catholic Womenpriests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Womenpriests) have performed ceremonies they affirm as sacramental ordinations, with, reputedly, an ordaining male Catholic bishop in the first few instances, which, according to [canon law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canon_law), are both illicit and invalid and considered simulations[^cite_note-cann1379-424] of the sacrament of ordination.[^cite_note-RCWP-425][^cite_note-426] The [Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congregation_for_the_Doctrine_of_the_Faith) responded by issuing a statement clarifying that any Catholic bishops involved in ordination ceremonies for women, as well as the women themselves if they were Catholic, would automatically receive the penalty of [excommunication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excommunication) (*[latae sententiae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latae_sententiae)*, literally "with the sentence already applied", i.e. automatically), citing canon 1378 of canon law and other church laws.[^cite_note-427]

### Sexual abuse cases

Main article: [Catholic Church sexual abuse cases](158934.md)

From the 1990s the issue of [sexual abuse of minors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexual_abuse_of_minors) by Catholic clergy and other church members has become the subject of civil litigation, criminal prosecution, media coverage and public debate in [countries around the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_sex_abuse_cases_by_country). Many Catholic bishops and other officials had protected priests accused of sexual abuse, and transferred them to other assignments elsewhere, where they continued to commit sex crimes against children. There has been resultant extensive public criticism of the church.[^cite_note-428]

In response to the scandals, formal procedures have been established to help prevent abuse, encourage the reporting of any abuse that occurs and to handle such reports promptly, although groups representing victims have disputed their effectiveness.[^cite_note-429] In 2014, Pope Francis instituted the [Pontifical Commission for the Protection of Minors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontifical_Commission_for_the_Protection_of_Minors) for the safeguarding of minors.[^cite_note-hspo.b0199/00444.2014.03.22-430]

### Environmental

The church has also addressed stewardship of the natural environment, and its relationship to other social and theological teachings. In the document *[Laudato si'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laudato_si')*, dated 24 May 2015, Pope Francis critiques [consumerism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consumerism) and [irresponsible development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overdevelopment), and laments [environmental degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_degradation) and [climate change](5042951.md).[^cite_note-NYT-431] The pope expressed concern that the warming of the planet is a symptom of a greater problem: the developed world's indifference to the destruction of the planet as humans pursue short-term economic gains.[^cite_note-432]

[^cite_note-RCbishop-32]: &#x20;Example use of "Roman" Catholic by a bishop's conference: *The Baltimore Catechism*, an official catechism authorized by the Catholic bishops of the United States, states: "That is why we are called Roman Catholics; to show that we are united to the real successor of St Peter" (Question 118) and refers to the church as the "Roman Catholic Church" under Questions 114 and 131 ([Baltimore Catechism).](http://www.cin.org/users/james/ebooks/master/baltimore/bcreed09.htm) [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20150923203520/http://www.cin.org/users/james/ebooks/master/baltimore/bcreed09.htm) 23 September 2015 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine)

[^cite_note-137]: &#x20;While ruling contraception to be prohibited, Pope Paul VI did, however, consider natural family planning methods to be morally permissible if used with just cause.

[^cite_note-166]: &#x20;According to Catholic teaching, Jesus Christ is the 'invisible Head' of the Church<sup>[\[155\]](#cite_note-161)</sup><sup>[\[156\]](#cite_note-162)</sup><sup>[\[157\]](#cite_note-163)</sup> while the pope is the 'visible Head'.<sup>[\[158\]](#cite_note-164)</sup><sup>[\[159\]](#cite_note-165)</sup>

[^cite_note-174]: &#x20;The last resignation occurred on 28 February 2013, when [Pope Benedict XVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XVI) retired, citing ill health in his advanced age. The next most recent resignation occurred in 1415, as part of the [Council of Constance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Constance)'s resolution of the [Avignon Papacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avignon_Papacy).<sup>[\[166\]](#cite_note-duffy415-173)</sup>

[^cite_note-212]: &#x20;In 1992, the Vatican clarified the 1983 Code of Canon Law removed the requirement that altar servers be male; permission to use female altar servers within a diocese is at the discretion of the bishop.<sup>[\[203\]](#cite_note-Apostalicae86-211)</sup>

[^cite_note-284]: &#x20;Other councils that addressed the sacraments include the [Second Council of Lyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Council_of_Lyon) (1274); [Council of Florence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Florence) (1439); as well as the [Council of Trent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Trent) (1547)<sup>[\[274\]](#cite_note-1113_14-283)</sup>

[^cite_note-340]: &#x20;Marriages involving unbaptized individuals are considered valid, but not sacramental. While sacramental marriages are insoluble, non-sacramental marriages may be dissolved under certain situations, such as a desire to marry a Catholic, under [Pauline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pauline_privilege) or [Petrine privilege](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrine_privilege).<sup>[\[328\]](#cite_note-Gantley-338)</sup><sup>[\[329\]](#cite_note-Doors-339)</sup>

[^cite_note-351]: &#x20;The Divine Worship variant of the Roman Rite differs from the "Anglican Use" variant, which was introduced in 1980 for the few United States parishes established in accordance with a [pastoral provision](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastoral_provision) for former members of the [Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(United_States\)) (the American branch of the Anglican Communion). Both uses adapted Anglican liturgical traditions for use within the Catholic Church.

[^cite_note-385]: &#x20;This part of the *Catechism* was quoted by Pope Francis in a 2013 press interview in which he remarked, when asked about an individual: "I think that when you encounter a person like this \[the individual he was asked about], you must make a distinction between the fact of a person being gay from the fact of being a lobby, because lobbies, all are not good. That is bad. If a person is gay and seeks the Lord and has good will, well who am I to judge them?"<sup>[\[369\]](#cite_note-381)</sup> This remark and others made in the same interview were seen as a change in the tone, but not in the substance of the teaching of the church,<sup>[\[370\]](#cite_note-382)</sup> which includes opposition to [same-sex marriage](92656.md).<sup>[\[371\]](#cite_note-1601_05-383)</sup><sup>[\[372\]](#cite_note-384)</sup>

[^cite_note-394]: &#x20;With regard to divorce in the United States, according to the Barna Group, among all who have been married, 33% have been divorced at least once; among American Catholics, 28% (the study did not track religious annulments).<sup>[\[380\]](#cite_note-393)</sup>

[^cite_note-403]: &#x20;Regarding use of [natural family planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_family_planning), in 2002, 24% of the U.S. population identified as Catholic,<sup>[\[387\]](#cite_note-adherents-401)</sup> but according to a 2002 study by the [Centers for Disease Control and Prevention](6811.md), of sexually active Americans avoiding pregnancy, only 1.5% were using NFP.<sup>[\[388\]](#cite_note-cdc-402)</sup>
